Image name: POGBb_task2_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are E and I. The goal is to disconnect them by removing the minimum number of white nodes.

2.  **Explore Paths:** I will systematically identify distinct paths between E and I. To find the minimum cut, I will look for node-disjoint paths. By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove (vertex cut) is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between the two target nodes.

    Let's find node-disjoint paths (paths that do not share any intermediate nodes):

    *   **Path 1:** E -> M -> J -> P -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J, P}

    *   **Path 2:** E -> B -> C -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1, as {M, J, P} and {B, C} have no common nodes.

    *   **Path 3:** E -> F -> X -> K -> G -> H -> I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, X, K, G, H}
        *   This path is node-disjoint from Path 1 ({M, J, P}) and Path 2 ({B, C}), as {F, X, K, G, H} has no common nodes with either of the previous sets.

3.  **Determine Minimum Cut Size:** I have identified three node-disjoint paths between E and I. According to Menger's theorem, this means that at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E from I.

4.  **Identify a Candidate Cut Set of Size 3:**
    The three paths identified above end with connections to I through nodes P, C, and H respectively. These are the direct neighbors of I.
    *   Path 1 uses P to connect to I.
    *   Path 2 uses C to connect to I.
    *   Path 3 uses H to connect to I.

    If I remove the nodes {P, C, H}, then I will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, as these are its only direct connections.

5.  **Verify the Cut Set [P, C, H]:**
    *   Removing P breaks Path 1 (E-M-J-P-I) and any other path going through P (e.g., E-M-J-D-P-I, E-B-N-Y-O-P-I).
    *   Removing C breaks Path 2 (E-B-C-I) and any other path going through C (e.g., E-B-N-C-I).
    *   Removing H breaks Path 3 (E-F-X-K-G-H-I) and any other path going through H (e.g., E-B-H-I, E-M-L-F-X-K-G-H-I).

    Since P, C, and H are the only nodes directly connected to I, removing them ensures that no path can reach I. This set of 3 nodes successfully disconnects E and I.

6.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 3. I also found a cut set of size 3 ([P, C, H]) that achieves this disconnection. Therefore, this is the minimum.

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [P, C, H]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}

Image name: POGBa_task3_gt3_PAIR4
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are H and B. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H and B.

1.  **Identify the direct connection:** There is a direct edge (H, B) between the two target nodes. This is one path.

2.  **Explore alternative paths:** I need to find all other paths between H and B that do not use the direct (H, B) edge. These paths must originate from H through its other neighbors (G or I) and eventually reach B through its other neighbors (E, C, or N).

    Let's list paths from H to B, trying to find edge-disjoint paths to apply Menger's Theorem (the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths).

    *   **Path 1 (P1):** H - B
        Edges: `{(H, B)}`

    *   **Path 2 (P2):** H - G - F - E - B
        Edges: `{(H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B)}`
        This path is edge-disjoint from P1.

    *   **Path 3 (P3):** H - I - C - B
        Edges: `{(H, I), (I, C), (C, B)}`
        This path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.

3.  **Verify edge-disjointness:**
    *   P1 uses only (H, B).
    *   P2 uses (H, G), (G, F), (F, E), (E, B). None of these are (H, B).
    *   P3 uses (H, I), (I, C), (C, B). None of these are (H, B), nor are they any of the edges in P2.
    Therefore, P1, P2, and P3 are three distinct edge-disjoint paths between H and B.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and B is at least 3.

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects H and B.
    Consider the edges directly connected to H: (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    If I remove these three edges:
    *   Removing (H, B) breaks the direct path P1.
    *   Removing (H, G) breaks any path starting H-G-... (like P2).
    *   Removing (H, I) breaks any path starting H-I-... (like P3).
    Since any path from H to B must start with one of these three edges, removing them will break all paths between H and B.

6.  **Conclusion:** I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, which means the minimum cut size is at least 3. I have also found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects H and B. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]
--- Ground Truth ---

Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are H and O. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect H and O. I cannot remove H or O.

2.  **Analyze direct connections from H and O:**
    *   Neighbors of H: G, B, I
    *   Neighbors of O: C, P, Y

3.  **Explore paths from H to O:** I will systematically trace paths from H to O.
    *   **Paths starting H -> G:**
        *   H -> G -> X -> K -> M -> L -> E -> B -> C -> O
        *   H -> G -> X -> F -> E -> B -> C -> O
        *   H -> G -> F -> E -> B -> C -> O
        (All paths through G eventually pass through E, then B, then C to reach O.)

    *   **Paths starting H -> B:**
        *   H -> B -> C -> O
        *   H -> B -> N -> P -> O
        *   H -> B -> E -> F -> G -> ... (This path eventually leads back to E, then B, then C to O, so it's covered by the H->B->E->B->C->O structure or H->G->... paths)

    *   **Paths starting H -> I:**
        *   H -> I -> P -> O
        *   H -> I -> J -> P -> O
        *   H -> I -> C -> O
        *   H -> I -> J -> M -> L -> E -> B -> C -> O
        *   H -> I -> J -> M -> E -> B -> C -> O

4.  **Determine if a single node removal is sufficient:**
    *   If I remove G: Paths H->B->...O and H->I->...O remain.
    *   If I remove B: Paths H->G->...O and H->I->...O remain.
    *   If I remove I: Paths H->G->...O and H->B->...O remain.
    *   If I remove C: Paths H->B->N->P->O and H->I->P->O remain.
    *   If I remove P: Paths H->B->C->O and H->I->C->O remain.
    *   If I remove E: Paths H->B->C->O and H->I->P->O remain.
    *   Removing any other single node (X, K, F, M, L, J, N, D, Y) would leave multiple paths intact.
    Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Search for a cut set of size 2:**
    I need to find two nodes whose removal breaks all paths identified in step 3.
    Let's consider removing nodes that are "bottlenecks" or "bridges" between H's side and O's side. The direct neighbors of H (G, B, I) are good candidates to consider.

    *   **Consider removing B and I:**
        *   If **B** is removed:
            *   All paths starting H -> B are broken.
            *   Paths H -> G -> ... -> E -> B -> C -> O are broken because B is removed. (E is connected to B, but B is gone, so E cannot reach C or N to get to O).
        *   If **I** is removed:
            *   All paths starting H -> I are broken.

        Let's verify this cut set [B, I]:
        1.  H is now only connected to G.
        2.  From G, we can reach X, K, F, M, L, E.
        3.  Now, I need to check if any of these nodes (G, X, K, F, M, L, E) can reach O (which is connected to C, P, Y).
            *   G, X, K, F, L are not connected to C, P, Y.
            *   M is connected to J. J is connected to I, P, M. Since I and P are removed (P is not removed by [B,I], but I is), J cannot reach O via I. J is connected to P. So, if P is still there, J can reach P.
                *   Wait, if I is removed, then J cannot reach P via I. J is directly connected to P. So, M -> J -> P -> O is a path.
                *   My previous analysis of [B,I] was flawed. Let's re-evaluate.

    *   **Re-evaluating [B, I] as a cut set:**
        If B and I are removed:
        1.  H -> G:
            *   G -> X -> K -> M -> L -> E. E is connected to M, L, F. E was connected to B, but B is removed. So E cannot reach C or N. No path to O.
            *   G -> X -> F -> E. Same as above.
            *   G -> F -> E. Same as above.
            So, all paths starting H -> G are broken.
        2.  H -> B: (Broken by removing B)
        3.  H -> I: (Broken by removing I)

        This seems correct. Let's check the path M -> J -> P -> O.
        This path starts from M. Can H reach M? Yes, H -> G -> X -> K -> M.
        So, H -> G -> X -> K -> M -> J -> P -> O.
        This path is still active if only B and I are removed.
        Therefore, [B, I] is NOT a cut set. My previous verification was incomplete.

    *   **Consider removing C and P:**
        *   If **C** is removed:
            *   Paths H -> B -> C -> O are broken.
            *   Paths H -> I -> C -> O are broken.
            *   Paths H -> G -> ... -> E -> B -> C -> O are broken.
        *   If **P** is removed:
            *   Paths H -> B -> N -> P -> O are broken.
            *   Paths H -> I -> P -> O are broken.
            *   Paths H -> I -> J -> P -> O are broken.

        Let's verify this cut set [C, P]:
        1.  From H -> G:
            *   H -> G -> X -> K -> M -> L -> E -> B. B is connected to H, E, C, N. Since C is removed, B cannot reach O via C. B is connected to N. N is connected to B, C, P. Since C and P are removed, N cannot reach O. So, paths from G to O are broken.
            *   H -> G -> X -> F -> E -> B. Same as above.
            *   H -> G -> F -> E -> B. Same as above.
            So, all paths starting H -> G are broken.
        2.  From H -> B:
            *   H -> B -> C (broken by C).
            *   H -> B -> N. N is connected to B, C, P. Since C and P are removed, N cannot reach O. So, paths H->B->N->...O are broken.
            *   H -> B -> E -> ... (leads to G, M, L, F, which cannot reach O without C or P).
            So, all paths starting H -> B are broken.
        3.  From H -> I:
            *   H -> I -> P (broken by P).
            *   H -> I -> J -> P (broken by P).
            *   H -> I -> C (broken by C).
            *   H -> I -> J -> M -> L -> E -> B. B cannot reach O (C and P removed).
            *   H -> I -> J -> M -> E -> B. B cannot reach O (C and P removed).
            So, all paths starting H -> I are broken.

        This verification confirms that removing **C** and **P** successfully disconnects H and O. This is a cut set of size 2.

Image name: PG_task3_gt3_PAIR5

Model: gemini-2.5-flash

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H (black) and E (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between H and E.

First, I identify all direct connections from H and to E:
Edges connected to H: (H, I), (H, X), (H, G)
Edges connected to E: (B, E), (F, E), (L, E)

I will systematically explore paths from H to E and identify critical edges.

**Analysis of paths from H:**
All paths originating from H must start with one of the edges (H, I), (H, X), or (H, G).

**Analysis of paths to E:**
All paths terminating at E must end with one of the edges (B, E), (F, E), or (L, E).

**Attempting a cut of size 1:**
- If I remove (H, I), paths like H-X-K-F-E or H-G-F-E still exist.
- If I remove (H, X), paths like H-I-C-B-E or H-G-F-E still exist.
- If I remove (H, G), paths like H-I-C-B-E or H-X-K-F-E still exist.
- If I remove (B, E), paths like H-I-J-M-L-E or H-X-K-F-E still exist.
- If I remove (F, E), paths like H-I-C-B-E or H-I-J-M-L-E still exist.
- If I remove (L, E), paths like H-I-C-B-E or H-X-K-F-E still exist.
Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

**Attempting a cut of size 2:**
I need to find two edges whose removal disconnects H and E. I will look for "bottleneck" edges that are part of many paths.

Let's categorize the paths based on their initial edge from H:

**Category 1: Paths starting with (H, I)**
These paths proceed from H to I, then branch out. Examples:
- H-I-C-B-E
- H-I-C-N-B-E
- H-I-J-M-L-E
- H-I-J-P-O-B-E
All paths in this category are cut if the edge (H, I) is removed.

**Category 2: Paths starting with (H, X) or (H, G)**
These paths proceed from H to X or H to G. Let's trace where they lead:
- From X, one can go to K or G.
- From G, one can go to F, K, or X.
- From K, one can go to F or G.
Notice that any path starting from H via X or G will eventually reach node F (e.g., H-X-K-F, H-X-G-F, H-G-F, H-G-K-F, H-G-X-K-F). From F, the only way to reach E is through the edge (F, E).
Therefore, all paths starting with (H, X) or (H, G) must eventually use the edge (F, E) to reach E.

**Proposed Cut Set:**
Based on the above analysis, if I remove:
1.  The edge (H, I): This cuts off all paths that start from H and go through I (Category 1).
2.  The edge (F, E): This cuts off all paths that start from H and go through X or G, as they all converge to F and then to E via (F, E) (Category 2).

Let's verify this cut set: [(H, I), (F, E)].
- If (H, I) is removed, no path can start H-I-...
- If (F, E) is removed, no path can end ...-F-E.

Are there any remaining paths?
- Any path from H must start with (H, I), (H, X), or (H, G).
- Paths starting with (H, I) are broken by removing (H, I).
- Paths starting with (H, X) or (H, G) must eventually pass through F to reach E (as established above). Since (F, E) is removed, these paths are also broken.

Thus, removing the edges (H, I) and (F, E) completely disconnects H from E. This is a cut set of size 2. Since I've already determined that a cut of size 1 is not possible, 2 is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(H, I), (F, E)]

    *   Other nodes like F, G, K, X, P, D, O, Y are not central enough to disconnect M and B by themselves, as they only affect a subset of paths. For example, removing P would cut paths like M-J-P-C-B, but M-J-I-B and M-E-H-B would remain.

        *   This means M is completely isolated from the rest of the graph. No path can extend from M to any other node, including B.

Image name: SEIS_task1_gt3_PAIR3
Task: task1_shortest_path_length
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning. The two black nodes are labeled L and I. I will perform a breadth-first search (BFS) starting from node L to find the shortest path to node I.

1.  **Initialize:**
    *   Queue: `[(L, [L])]` (Node, Path)
    *   Visited: `{}`

2.  **Level 0:**
    *   Dequeue `(L, [L])`.
    *   Add L to Visited.

3.  **Level 1:**
    *   Neighbors of L: E, M.
    *   Enqueue `(E, [L, E])`.
    *   Enqueue `(M, [L, M])`.
    *   Visited: `{L, E, M}`.
    *   No target node I found yet.

4.  **Level 2:**
    *   Dequeue `(E, [L, E])`.
        *   Neighbors of E: L (visited), M (visited), F, G, B.
        *   Enqueue `(F, [L, E, F])`.
        *   Enqueue `(G, [L, E, G])`.
        *   Enqueue `(B, [L, E, B])`.
        *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, G, B}`.
    *   Dequeue `(M, [L, M])`.
        *   Neighbors of M: L (visited), E (visited), F (already in queue with a path of same length), J.
        *   Enqueue `(J, [L, M, J])`.
        *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, G, B, J}`.
    *   No target node I found yet.

5.  **Level 3:**
    *   Dequeue `(F, [L, E, F])`.
        *   Neighbors of F: M (visited), E (visited), X, K, G (visited).
        *   Enqueue `(X, [L, E, F, X])`.
        *   Enqueue `(K, [L, E, F, K])`.
        *   Visited: `{L, E, M, F, G, B, J, X, K}`.
    *   Dequeue `(G, [L, E, G])`.
        *   Neighbors of G: E (visited), F (visited), X (visited), K (visited), H, B (visited).
        *   Enqueue `(H, [L, E, G, H])`.

Image name: FD-FR_task3_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are H and B. The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect H and B.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between H and B.**
    To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. Let's find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** H - B
        Edges: `{(H, B)}`

    *   **Path 2:** H - E - B
        Edges: `{(H, E), (E, B)}`
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** H - I - B
        Edges: `{(H, I), (I, B)}`
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

    *   **Path 4:** H - F - E - I - C - B
        Edges: `{(H, F), (F, E), (E, I), (I, C), (C, B)}`
        Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from the previous three:
        *   No overlap with Path 1 `{(H, B)}`.
        *   No overlap with Path 2 `{(H, E), (E, B)}` (e.g., (H,F) vs (H,E), (F,E) vs (E,B)).
        *   No overlap with Path 3 `{(H, I), (I, B)}` (e.g., (H,F) vs (H,I), (F,E) vs (I,B)).
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, Path 2, and Path 3.

    We have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between H and B. Therefore, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 4 edges must be removed to disconnect H and B.

2.  **Find a cut set of 4 edges.**
    Now, we need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal will disconnect H and B. A valid cut set must contain at least one edge from each of the identified edge-disjoint paths.

    Let's consider the following set of edges:
    `S = [(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)]`

    Let's verify if this cut set disconnects H and B:
    *   `(H, B)` cuts Path 1.
    *   `(H, E)` cuts Path 2.
    *   `(H, I)` cuts Path 3.
    *   `(F, E)` cuts Path 4 (as `(F, E)` is an edge in Path 4).

    All four identified edge-disjoint paths are broken by this cut set. Now, let's systematically check if any other path exists after removing these edges.

    After removing `(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)`:
    *   Node H is now connected only to G and F.
    *   Node B is now connected to E, I, C, N.

    Let's trace paths from H:
    *   **From H to G:** G is connected to F, X, K.
        *   G -> F: F is connected to G, X, K. The edge `(F, E)` has been removed, so F cannot reach E. Since E is the only gateway from F to B's side of the graph (via E, I, C, N), no path from H-G-F can reach B.
        *   G -> X: X is connected to F, K. These nodes are not connected to B's side.
        *   G -> K: K is connected to F, X. These nodes are not connected to B's side.
    *   **From H to F:** F is connected to G, X, K. The edge `(F, E)` has been removed, so F cannot reach E. No path from H-F can reach B.

    Since H can no longer reach E or I (due to the removal of `(H, E)`, `(H, I)`, and `(F, E)`), and the direct connection `(H, B)` is removed, H is completely disconnected from B.

3.  **Conclusion:**
    We have established that at least 4 edges must be removed, and we have found a set of 4 edges that successfully disconnects H and B. Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, B), (H, E), (H, I), (F, E)]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are O (black) and H (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect O from H.

First, I identify all possible paths between O and H. I'll group them by their general "route" to H. The node H has four direct neighbors: I, C, X, and G. Any path from O to H must pass through one of these four nodes.

Let's categorize the paths based on which of H's neighbors they use:

**Group 1: Paths to H via I**
*   Path 1: O - P - D - J - I - H
*   Path 2: O - M - J - I - H
Both of these paths share the edge (J, I). Removing (J, I) would disconnect all paths in this group from H.

**Group 2: Paths to H via C**
*   Path 3: O - P - Y - N - C - H
*   Path 4: O - N - B - C - H
Both of these paths share the edge (C, H). Removing (C, H) would disconnect all paths in this group from H.

**Group 3: Paths to H via X**
*   Path 5: O - N - B - E - F - X - H
*   Path 6: O - M - L - E - F - X - H
Both of these paths share the edge (F, X) (and also (X, H)).

**Group 4: Paths to H via G**
*   Path 7: O - N - B - E - F - G - H
*   Path 8: O - M - L - E - F - G - H
Both of these paths share the edge (F, G) (and also (G, H)).

Now, let's look for a minimal set of edges to cut all these paths.
Notice that Group 3 (paths to X) and Group 4 (paths to G) both pass through the segment E - F.
If we remove the edge (E, F), all paths in Group 3 and Group 4 are simultaneously cut. This accounts for 1 edge.

After removing (E, F), we still need to cut paths in Group 1 and Group 2.
To cut Group 1 paths (to I), we can remove the edge (J, I). This accounts for another 1 edge.
To cut Group 2 paths (to C), we can remove the edge (C, H). This accounts for a third 1 edge.

So, the cut set [(E, F), (J, I), (C, H)] consists of 3 edges. Let's verify that these three edges indeed disconnect O from H:
1.  Removing (E, F) breaks all paths that go through E to F, which includes all paths to X-H and G-H.
2.  Removing (J, I) breaks all paths that go through J to I, which includes all paths to I-H.
3.  Removing (C, H) breaks all paths that go through C to H, which includes all paths to C-H.
Since all paths from O to H must pass through one of these three "bottleneck" points (E-F, J-I, or C-H), removing these three edges ensures complete disconnection.

Now, I need to confirm that 3 is the *minimum* number of edges.
Consider the three "bottleneck" points identified:
*   The "I-branch" (paths to H via I, cut by (J, I)).
*   The "C-branch" (paths to H via C, cut by (C, H)).
*   The "F-branch" (paths to H via X or G, cut by (E, F)).

These three branches are largely independent in terms of their critical edges:
*   If we remove only (J, I) and (C, H), paths through the F-branch (e.g., O-N-B-E-F-X-H) still exist.
*   If we remove only (J, I) and (E, F), paths through the C-branch (e.g., O-N-B-C-H) still exist.
*   If we remove only (C, H) and (E, F), paths through the I-branch (e.g., O-M-J-I-H) still exist.

Since removing any two of these edges leaves at least one path open, it is impossible to disconnect O and H with only 2 edges. Therefore, 3 edges is the minimum required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, F), (J, I), (C, H)]

            *   However, the path L-M-J is still active. So, this is not a valid cut set.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J:**
    I will list several distinct paths to understand the connectivity.
    *   Path 1: E - M - J
    *   Path 2: E - F - K - J
    *   Path 3: E - G - H - I - J
    *   Path 4: E - B - C - O - P - J

2.  **Check for edge-disjoint paths:**
    To determine the minimum cut size, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between E and J.
    *   Path 1: E - M - J (Edges: (E, M), (M, J))
    *   Path 2: E - F - K - J (Edges: (E, F), (F, K), (K, J))
    *   Path 3: E - G - H - I - J (Edges: (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J))
    *   Path 4: E - B - C - O - P - J (Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J))

    Let's examine the edges used in these four paths:
    *   P1 uses (E, M), (M, J)
    *   P2 uses (E, F), (F, K), (K, J)
    *   P3 uses (E, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)
    *   P4 uses (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Upon inspection, all edges listed across these four paths are unique to their respective paths. This means these four paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

3.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Menger's theorem states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges in an edge cut that disconnects them. Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 4.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 4:**
    Now I need to find a set of 4 edges whose removal disconnects E and J. A common strategy is to identify all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that also lead to the other target node.
    Consider the edges directly connected to J: (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J), (D, J).
    *   Node D is a leaf node connected only to J. There are no paths from E to D, so removing (D, J) is not necessary to disconnect E from J.
    *   The remaining edges are (M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J). If these four edges are removed, J will be disconnected from M, K, I, and P. Since all paths from E to J must pass through one of these nodes (M, K, I, P) before reaching J, removing these four edges will sever all connections between E and J.

    Let's verify this cut set: [(M, J), (K, J), (I, J), (P, J)].
    *   Any path E-...-M-J is cut by (M, J).
    *   Any path E-...-K-J is cut by (K, J). (This includes paths like E-F-K-J, E-F-X-K-J, E-G-K-J).
    *   Any path E-...-I-J is cut by (I, J). (This includes paths like E-G-H-I-J, E-B-C-I-J, E-B-N-C-I-J).
    *   Any path E-...-P-J is cut by (P, J). (This includes paths like E-B-C-O-P-J, E-B-N-C-O-P-J, E-B-N-Y-O-P-J).

    All paths from E to J are indeed broken by removing these 4 edges.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since the minimum cut is at least 4 (from Menger's theorem) and I have found a cut set of size 4, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.

Step-by-step reasoning:

I will use Menger's theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between those two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are N and H.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between N and H:** I will systematically search for paths from N to H, ensuring that each new path does not share any edges with previously found paths.

        N - B - H
        Edges in Path 1: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`

        N - O - C - I - H
        Edges in Path 2: `(N, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)`
        Checking for shared edges with Path 1: None. Path 2 is edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    *   **Path 3:** Next, I search for a path from N to H that does not use any edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far: `(N, B)`, `(B, H)`, `(N, O)`, `(O, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, H)`.

        N - Y - O ...
        From O, I cannot use `(O, C)` (used in Path 2). I can use `(O, P)`.
        N - Y - O - P ...
        From P, I cannot use `(P, I)` if it leads to `(I, H)` (used in Path 2). So I must find an alternative route from P. P is connected to D and I. If I go P-I, then I-H uses an edge from Path 2. So I must go P-D.
        N - Y - O - P - D - J - M - L - E - F - K - G - H
        Edges in Path 3: `(N, Y)`, `(Y, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, D)`, `(D, J)`, `(J, M)`, `(M, L)`, `(L, E)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, K)`, `(K, G)`, `(G, H)`
        Checking for shared edges with Path 1 and Path 2: None. Path 3 is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:** I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect N and H is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 3.

4.  **Identify a minimal cut set:** To disconnect H from N, all paths leading to H must be cut. H is directly connected to nodes B, G, and I. Therefore, removing the edges connecting H to these three nodes will isolate H from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of E:** E is connected to L, M, F, and B.
2.  **Identify direct neighbors of I:** I is connected to J, P, C, and H.

Let's analyze the paths from E to I. All paths from E must start by going through one of its neighbors: L, M, F, or B.

*   **Paths through L or M:**
    *   E -> L -> M -> F -> ... -> I
    *   E -> M -> F -> ... -> I
    Notice that both L and M are connected to F. In fact, L is connected to E, M, F. M is connected to E, L, F. This means any path from E that goes through L or M *must* eventually pass through F to reach any other part of the graph (other than E itself).
    So, all paths starting E -> L or E -> M will eventually pass through F.

*   **Paths through F:**
    *   E -> F -> G -> H -> I
    *   E -> F -> K -> J -> I
    *   E -> F -> X -> K -> J -> I
    These paths directly use F.

*   **Paths through B:**
    *   E -> B -> H -> I
    *   E -> B -> C -> I
    *   E -> B -> N -> C -> I
    *   E -> B -> N -> O -> P -> I
    *   E -> B -> N -> O -> C -> I
    *   E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> P -> I
    *   E -> B -> N -> Y -> O -> C -> I
    These paths directly use B.

From this analysis, we can see that all paths from E to I must pass through either node F or node B.
*   If a path starts E -> L or E -> M, it must then go through F to continue towards I.
*   If a path starts E -> F, it directly uses F.
*   If a path starts E -> B, it directly uses B.

Therefore, F and B are the two critical "bottleneck" nodes that separate E from the rest of the graph that connects to I.

1.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If we remove F, all paths going through F (including those via L and M) are broken. However, paths through B (e.g., E -> B -> H -> I) would still exist. So, F alone is not enough.
    *   If we remove B, all paths going through B are broken. However, paths through F (e.g., E -> F -> G -> H -> I) would still exist. So, B alone is not enough.
    Since no single node can disconnect E from I, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

2.  **Test for a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider removing both F and B.
        *   Removing F breaks all paths that go E -> F, E -> L -> M -> F, or E -> M -> F.
        *   Removing B breaks all paths that go E -> B.
    Since all paths from E to I must pass through either F or B, removing both F and B will sever all connections between E and I.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set is [F, B].

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [F, B]

    *   Visited: `{}`

        *   Neighbors of J: I (visited), D, P, M.

    *   Visited: `{I, J, H, C}`

        *   Neighbors of D: J (visited), P. (P is already in the queue from a shorter path from I, so we don't add it again).
        *   Mark D as visited.

    Any path from B to M must pass through one of B's neighbors (H, C, N) and one of M's neighbors (E, L, J).
    Let's categorize paths based on which neighbor of M they use:

    *   **Paths through E to M:**
        *   B - H - E - M
        *   B - H - G - F - E - M
        To break all paths going through E, we must remove E (or H, G, F, but E is the direct link to M). Removing E is the most direct way to cut all paths ending in E-M.

    *   **Paths through J to M:**
        There are many paths that lead to J, and then to M. Examples include:
        *   B - H - I - J - M
        *   B - H - I - P - J - M
        *   B - C - I - J - M
        *   B - C - O - P - J - M
        *   B - N - O - P - J - M
        *   B - N - C - I - J - M
        All these paths share J as the final node before M. Therefore, removing J would break all paths that use J to reach M.

    *   **Paths through L to M:**
        Node L is only connected to M. There are no paths from B to L (and then to M) without passing through M itself. So, L does not provide an alternative route from B to M.

4.  **Evaluate Single Node Removal:**
    *   If I remove H: Paths like B-C-I-J-M or B-N-O-P-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove C: Paths like B-H-E-M or B-N-O-P-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove N: Paths like B-H-E-M or B-C-I-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove E: Paths like B-H-I-J-M or B-C-O-P-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove J: Paths like B-H-E-M or B-H-G-F-E-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove I: Paths like B-H-E-M or B-C-O-P-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    *   If I remove P: Paths like B-H-E-M or B-H-I-J-M still exist. (Not enough)
    Since removing any single node does not disconnect B and M, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Evaluate Two Node Removal (Candidate Cut Set):**
    Based on the analysis in step 3, we identified that all paths to M either go through E or through J.
    *   Removing **E** will break all paths that use E to reach M.
    *   Removing **J** will break all paths that use J to reach M.
    *   Since M is also connected to L, and L is an isolated node from the perspective of B (L is only connected to M), removing E and J will completely isolate M from any path originating from B.

    Therefore, removing both E and J will disconnect B and M. This is a cut set of size 2: {E, J}.

6.  **Verify Minimum:**
    We've established that a single node removal is insufficient. We found a valid cut set of size 2. Thus, 2 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

Image name: SEIS_task2_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My task is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

I will identify all distinct paths from E to I and look for "bottleneck" nodes. A common strategy for this type of problem is to look for vertex-disjoint paths. The maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of nodes needed to disconnect them (Menger's Theorem for vertex connectivity).

Let's identify potential vertex-disjoint paths from E to I:

1.  **Path 1 (via G and H):** E - G - H - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {G, H}

2.  **Path 2 (via B and C):** E - B - C - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {B, C}

3.  **Path 3 (via M and J):** E - M - J - I
    *   Intermediate nodes: {M, J}

Let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (excluding the start and end nodes E and I):
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {G, H}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {B, C}.
*   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {M, J}.

These three sets of intermediate nodes are mutually disjoint. This means that these three paths are vertex-disjoint.
According to Menger's Theorem, if there are 3 vertex-disjoint paths, then at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect E from I.

Now, I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects E from I. Based on the identified disjoint paths, removing one node from each path's intermediate nodes would be a good candidate.
Let's try removing {G, B, M}:

1.  **Remove G:** This breaks Path 1 (E-G-H-I). It also breaks any other paths that rely on G, such as E-F-K-G-H-I, E-F-X-K-G-H-I, and E-F-G-H-I. Essentially, all paths from E that go through G are severed.

2.  **Remove B:** This breaks Path 2 (E-B-C-I). It also breaks other paths through B, such as E-B-N-C-I, E-B-O-I, and E-B-N-Y-O-I. All paths from E that go through B are severed.

3.  **Remove M:** This breaks Path 3 (E-M-J-I). It also breaks other paths through M, such as E-M-J-P-I, E-M-J-P-O-I, and E-L-M-J-I (since L connects to M). All paths from E that go through M (or L then M) are severed.

After removing G, B, and M, let's verify if any path remains from E to I:
*   E's direct neighbors are L, M, F, G, B.
*   Nodes M, G, B are removed.
*   Remaining direct neighbors of E are L and F.
    *   If E goes to L: The only path from L is L-M. But M is removed, so this path is broken.
    *   If E goes to F: From F, it can go to X, K, or G.
        *   F-X-K: From K, it can go to G. But G is removed, so this path is broken.
        *   F-K: From K, it can go to G. But G is removed, so this path is broken.
        *   F-G: But G is removed, so this path is broken.

Since all paths from E's remaining neighbors (L and F) are blocked by the removal of G or M, there are no remaining paths from E to I.

Therefore, removing {G, B, M} successfully disconnects E from I. Since we established that at least 3 nodes are required, this set of 3 nodes is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [G, B, M]

Image name: PG_task3_gt2_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are N (black) and G (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect N from G. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's theorem, is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between N and G.

First, I will try to find the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths from N to G.

1.  **Identify a first path (P1):**
    Let's choose a relatively direct path: N - C - H - G.
    Edges used by P1: `{(N, C), (C, H), (H, G)}`

2.  **Identify a second path (P2) that is edge-disjoint from P1:**
    From N, we can go through B.
    Path: N - B - F - K - G.
    Edges used by P2: `{(N, B), (B, F), (F, K), (K, G)}`
    Checking for conflicts with P1:
    -   `(N, C)` vs `(N, B)`: Disjoint.
    -   `(C, H)` vs `(B, F)`: Disjoint.
    -   `(H, G)` vs `(F, K)`: Disjoint.
    -   `(H, G)` vs `(K, G)`: Disjoint.
    P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Identify a third path (P3) that is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:**
    From N, we still have outgoing edges `(N, Y)` and `(N, O)` available.
    Let's try starting with `(N, O)`:
    Path: N - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - X - G.
    Edges used by P3: `{(N, O), (O, P), (P, J), (J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G)}`
    Checking for conflicts with P1: `{(N, C), (C, H), (H, G)}`
    -   No common edges. P3 is disjoint from P1.
    Checking for conflicts with P2: `{(N, B), (B, F), (F, K), (K, G)}`
    -   No common edges. P3 is disjoint from P2.
    Therefore, P3 is edge-disjoint from both P1 and P2.

We have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and G:
*   P1: N - C - H - G
*   P2: N - B - F - K - G
*   P3: N - O - P - J - M - L - E - F - X - G

According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut) must be at least equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths (max-flow). Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum cut is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 3 edges that disconnects N from G.
A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
Node G is connected to X, K, and H.
If we remove the edges `(X, G)`, `(K, G)`, and `(H, G)`, node G will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

Let's verify this cut set: `{(X, G), (K, G), (H, G)}`.
1.  Removing `(X, G)` breaks any path that enters G via X.
2.  Removing `(K, G)` breaks any path that enters G via K.
3.  Removing `(H, G)` breaks any path that enters G via H.
Since X, K, and H are the only nodes directly connected to G, removing these three edges ensures that no path can reach G from any other node in the graph, including N.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are G and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G from I. This is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and I (Menger's Theorem).

First, I will identify all direct and indirect paths between G and I.
Nodes connected to G: X, F, H.
Nodes connected to I: H, C, P.

Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths:

**Path 1:** G - H - I
Edges: `(G, H)`, `(H, I)`

**Path 2:** G - F - E - B - C - I
Edges: `(G, F)`, `(F, E)`, `(E, B)`, `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`
This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses entirely different edges.

Now, I will attempt to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
Edges used in Path 1: `{(G, H), (H, I)}`
Edges used in Path 2: `{(G, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C), (C, I)}`

Let's try to construct a third path:
1.  Start from G. The only remaining unused edge from G is `(G, X)`. So, the path must start `G - X`.
2.  From X, there are two unused edges: `(X, K)` and `(X, F)`.
    *   If `G - X - F`: The edge `(F, E)` is used in Path 2, and `(F, G)` is used in Path 2. The only other edge from F is `(F, K)`. So, `G - X - F - K`. From K, the only edges are `(K, X)` (already used in this segment) and `(K, F)` (already used in this segment). This path is stuck and cannot reach I without using edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
    *   If `G - X - K`: From K, the only unused edge is `(K, F)`. So, `G - X - K - F`. From F, the edges `(F, E)` and `(F, G)` are used in Path 2. The only other edge from F is `(F, X)` (already used in this segment). This path is also stuck and cannot reach I without using edges from Path 1 or Path 2.

Since all attempts to find a third edge-disjoint path fail, it indicates that there are at most two edge-disjoint paths between G and I. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G and I is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 2.

Now, I need to find a cut set of 2 edges that disconnects G and I.
Let's consider the edges `(G, H)` and `(F, E)`.

1.  **Remove (G, H):** This breaks Path 1 (G - H - I) and any other path that directly uses the `(G, H)` connection.
2.  **Remove (F, E):** This breaks Path 2 (G - F - E - B - C - I) and any other path that relies on the `(F, E)` connection to move from the left side of the graph (G's vicinity) to the central part.

Let's verify if G is completely disconnected from I after removing `(G, H)` and `(F, E)`:
*   Node G's original neighbors: X, F, H.
*   After removing `(G, H)`, G's neighbors are: X, F.
*   Node F's original neighbors: X, K, G, E.
*   After removing `(F, E)`, F's neighbors are: X, K, G.
*   Node X's neighbors: K, F, G.
*   Node K's neighbors: X, F.

The nodes {G, X, K, F} now form a connected component. Let's check if there are any edges from this component to any other node in the graph (which could eventually lead to I).
*   From G: only to X, F (within the component).
*   From X: only to K, F, G (within the component).
*   From K: only to X, F (within the component).
*   From F: only to X, K, G (within the component).

Since there are no outgoing edges from the {G, X, K, F} component to any other part of the graph, G is completely isolated from I.

Therefore, removing edges `(G, H)` and `(F, E)` successfully disconnects G from I, and this is a minimal cut of size 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(G, H), (F, E)]

*   Path: M - L - E - B (This path also uses E to reach B)

**Category 2: Paths starting with (M, J)**
All paths from M that go through J will eventually reach B through a sequence of nodes like P, O, I, D, N, C, H. Examples include:
*   M - J - P - O - N - B

*   Path: M - L - E - B (This path eventually merges into Category 1 paths at node E)

It's clear that no single edge can disconnect M and B, as there are multiple distinct routes. For example, removing (M, E) would still leave paths via (M, J) and (M, L). Removing (E, B) would still leave paths via (M, J).

1.  **Cutting Category 1 and 3 paths (those involving E):**

*   The edge (E, B) breaks all paths that go through E to reach B (M-E-B and M-L-E-B).
*   The edge (M, J) breaks all paths that start from M via J (e.g., M-J-P-O-N-B, M-J-D-P-I-H-B, etc.).

*   E is connected to L, M, F, and B. Since (E, B) is removed, E cannot reach B.
*   Therefore, M cannot reach B through E or L.
*   Since (M, J) is removed, M cannot reach B through J.

Thus, removing the edges (M, J) and (E, B) completely disconnects M from B. This is a cut set of size 2.

Since I have established that a single edge is not sufficient, and I have found a cut set of 2 edges, this must be the minimum.

        *   E - L - M - F - K - G - H
        *   E - L - M - F - X - G - H
        These paths all pass through M and then F, so they are also covered by the "Paths through F" category.

    *   **Branch 1:** All paths that start with E -> B. This branch includes the direct E-B-H path and all paths that go E-B-...-I-H.
    *   **Branch 2:** All paths that start with E -> F (or E -> M -> F, or E -> L -> M -> F). These paths all eventually lead to G, which then connects to H.

6.  **Determine Minimum Cut Set:** To disconnect E and H, we must sever all paths in Branch 1 AND all paths in Branch 2.
    *   **To sever Branch 1 (E -> B -> ... -> H):** The most effective node to remove is B. Removing B breaks all connections from E that go through B, including the direct E-B-H path and all paths that would eventually reach I and then H.
    *   **To sever Branch 2 (E -> F -> ... -> G -> H):** We have two primary options:

        *   Remove G: This breaks all paths that lead to H via G (E-...-G-H). Since F, K, and X all connect to G on their way to H, removing G effectively cuts off this entire branch.

    *   We can remove either F or G to cut Branch 2.

*   If I remove node J, all paths in "Paths through J" are broken. However, paths in "Paths through E" would still exist (e.g., M -> E -> B). So, removing J alone is not sufficient.

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are X and C. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between X and C.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are X and C.

2.  **List direct connections for X:**
    *   X is connected to F.
    *   X is connected to K.
    *   X is connected to G.

3.  **List direct connections for C:**
    *   C is connected to B.
    *   C is connected to O.
    *   C is connected to I.
    *   C is connected to N.

4.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate X, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (X, F), (X, K), (X, G). This is a set of 3 edges.
    *   To isolate C, I would need to remove all edges connected to it: (C, B), (C, O), (C, I), (C, N). This is a set of 4 edges.
    *   Since 3 is less than 4, a cut of size 3 is a possible candidate for the minimum.

5.  **Attempt to find a smaller cut (e.g., 1 or 2 edges):**
    *   **Cut of size 1:** It's clear that removing any single edge will not disconnect X from C, as there are multiple alternative paths. For example, if (X, F) is removed, paths via (X, G) and (X, K) still exist.
    *   **Cut of size 2:** Let's try to find two edges that cut all paths.
        *   If we remove (X, F) and (X, G), paths via (X, K) still exist (e.g., X-K-J-P-O-C). So, this is not enough.
        *   If we remove (B, C) and (I, C), paths via (O, C) and (N, C) still exist (e.g., X-K-J-P-O-C). So, this is not enough.
        *   It seems unlikely that a cut of size 2 exists, given the number of distinct routes.

6.  **Apply Menger's Theorem (edge version):** The minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Let's try to find edge-disjoint paths from X to C.

    *   **Path 1:** X - F - E - B - C
        *   Edges: (X, F), (F, E), (E, B), (B, C)

    *   **Path 2:** X - G - H - I - C
        *   Edges: (X, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, C)

    *   **Path 3:** X - K - J - P - O - C
        *   Edges: (X, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C)

7.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   (X, F) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (X, G) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (X, K) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (F, E) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (G, H) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (K, J) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (E, B) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (H, I) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (J, P) is unique to Path 3.
    *   (B, C) is unique to Path 1.
    *   (I, C) is unique to Path 2.
    *   (O, C) is unique to Path 3.

    All edges in these three paths are distinct. Therefore, there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between X and C.

8.  **Conclusion:** Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges required to disconnect X and C must be at least 3. We have already identified a cut set of size 3 by removing all edges directly connected to X: [(X, F), (X, K), (X, G)]. This set successfully isolates X from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from C.

Image name: POGBa_task2_gt3_PAIR5
Task: task2_min_nodes_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning.
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are P and N. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect P and N.

2.  **List all direct neighbors of P and N (excluding each other):**
    *   Neighbors of P: D, J, O
    *   Neighbors of N: C, O, B

3.  **Identify potential bottlenecks and paths:**
    *   Notice that node O is a common neighbor to both P and N (P-O-N is a direct path).
    *   All paths from P must start through D, J, or O. Node D is a dead end as it has no connections leading towards N.
    *   All paths to N must end through C, O, or B.

4.  **Find node-disjoint paths:** To determine the minimum cut set size, I will use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent nodes is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between them.
    *   **Path 1:** P - O - N. (Intermediate node: O)
    *   **Path 2:** P - J - I - C - N. (Intermediate nodes: J, I, C)

    Let's check if these two paths are node-disjoint. The set of intermediate nodes for Path 1 is {O}. The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {J, I, C}. These two sets are disjoint. So, Path 1 and Path 2 are node-disjoint.

    Now, I need to determine if there is a third path that is node-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
    *   From P, the only neighbors are D, J, O.
    *   D is a dead end.
    *   O is used by Path 1.
    *   J is used by Path 2.
    Since all viable neighbors of P (J and O) are already used by Path 1 or Path 2, it is impossible to find a third path that is node-disjoint from both.

    Therefore, the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between P and N is 2. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect P and N is 2.

5.  **Identify a cut set of size 2:** Based on the two node-disjoint paths identified, a cut set of size 2 can be formed by picking one node from each path.
    *   From Path 1 (P-O-N), we can pick O.
    *   From Path 2 (P-J-I-C-N), we can pick J, I, or C.

    Let's test the cut set [O, J]:
    *   If node O is removed: The path P-O-N is broken. Also, P-O-C-N is broken.
    *   If node J is removed: All paths starting with P-J-... are broken. This includes P-J-I-C-N, P-J-I-H-B-N, P-J-M-E-B-N, and any other paths that branch from J.

    Since removing O breaks all paths through O, and removing J breaks all paths through J, and these two nodes (O and J) cover all initial branches from P (excluding the dead end D), the set [O, J] successfully disconnects P from N.

6.  **Verify no smaller cut set exists:** Since the maximum number of node-disjoint paths is 2, a cut set of size 1 is not possible.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [O, J]

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G (black) and N (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect G and N. This is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between G and N, according to Menger's Theorem.

I will identify three edge-disjoint paths between G and N. If I can find three such paths, then the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least three.

1.  **Path 1 (P1): G - H - B - N**
    Edges in P1: (G, H), (H, B), (B, N)

2.  **Path 2 (P2): G - E - M - J - P - O - N**
    Edges in P2: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, N)

3.  **Path 3 (P3): G - F - E - H - I - C - N**
    Edges in P3: (G, F), (F, E), (E, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)

Now, I will verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint (i.e., they do not share any common edges):
*   Edges of P1: (G,H), (H,B), (B,N)
*   Edges of P2: (G,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)
*   Edges of P3: (G,F), (F,E), (E,H), (H,I), (I,C), (C,N)

Comparing the edge sets:
*   No edge from P1 appears in P2 or P3.
*   No edge from P2 appears in P1 or P3.
*   No edge from P3 appears in P1 or P2.

Since I have found three edge-disjoint paths between G and N, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect them is at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of exactly 3 edges. A straightforward approach is to consider the edges directly connected to one of the target nodes. Node N has four direct connections: (N, B), (N, C), (N, O), and (N, Y).
If we remove the edges (N, B), (N, C), and (N, O), let's verify if N is disconnected from G.
*   Removing (N, B) breaks P1.
*   Removing (N, O) breaks P2.
*   Removing (N, C) breaks P3.

After removing these three edges, N is only connected to Y. Node Y is connected to N and O. Since (N, O) is also removed, Y becomes an isolated node connected only to N. There are no paths from G to Y. Therefore, removing these three edges completely disconnects N from G.

This cut set has a size of 3, which matches the minimum determined by the edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer:

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J and E. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J from E. This is a min-cut problem. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify potential cut sets:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node is to remove all edges directly connected to it.
    *   Node J has 4 direct connections: (J, M), (J, D), (J, P), (J, I). Removing these 4 edges would isolate J.
    *   Node E has 3 direct connections: (E, L), (E, B), (E, F). Removing these 3 edges would isolate E.
    Since 3 is less than 4, a cut set of size 3 is a candidate for the minimum.

2.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths:**
    To confirm if 3 is indeed the minimum, I will try to find 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E. If I can find 3 such paths, then the minimum cut must be at least 3.

    *   **Path 1:** J - M - L - E
        Edges in Path 1: `{(J, M), (M, L), (L, E)}`

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I need to find a path from J to E that does not use any of the edges from Path 1.
        J has other connections: (J, D), (J, P), (J, I).
        E has other connections: (E, B), (E, F).
        Let's try a path through I and B: J - I - C - B - E
        Edges in Path 2: `{(J, I), (I, C), (C, B), (B, E)}`
        Checking for shared edges with Path 1: There are no common edges. Path 1 and Path 2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I need to find a path from J to E that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far: `{(J, M), (M, L), (L, E), (J, I), (I, C), (C, B), (B, E)}`
        From J, I can still use (J, D) or (J, P).
        To E, I can still use (E, F).
        Let's try a path starting with (J, D) and ending with (F, E):
        J - D - P - Y - N - B - F - E
        Edges in Path 3: `{(J, D), (D, P), (P, Y), (Y, N), (N, B), (B, F), (F, E)}`
        Checking for shared edges with Path 1: No common edges.
        Checking for shared edges with Path 2: No common edges. Node B is present in both Path 2 and Path 3, but the edges connected to B are different: (C, B) and (B, E) for Path 2, and (N, B) and (B, F) for Path 3. Thus, no edge conflict at B.

3.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    I have successfully identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between J and E. This means, by Menger's Theorem, that at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect J and E.

4.  **Verify the cut set:**
    The cut set `[(E, L), (E, B), (E, F)]` consists of 3 edges. If these three edges are removed, node E becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Therefore, no path can exist between J and E.

Since I found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects the nodes, this is the minimum number of edges.

3.  **Define Sets S and T**:
    Let's define S as the set of nodes that are "closer" to G and form a connected component around G, and T as the rest of the nodes, which includes N.
    *   Let **S = {G, X, K, F, H}**. This set contains G and all nodes directly connected to G, and their immediate neighbors that are not clearly on the "path to N" yet.
    *   Let **T = {N, Y, O, C, B, I, P, J, D, L, M, E}**. This set contains N and all other nodes in the graph.

4.  **Identify Crossing Edges (Cut Set)**: Now, I will systematically list all edges that connect a node in set S to a node in set T.
    *   **From G**: G is connected to X, K, F, H. All of these nodes (X, K, F, H) are in S. So, no edges from G cross the boundary to T.
    *   **From X**: X is connected to G, K, F. All of these nodes (G, K, F) are in S. So, no edges from X cross the boundary to T.
    *   **From K**: K is connected to G, X, F. All of these nodes (G, X, F) are in S. So, no edges from K cross the boundary to T.
    *   **From F**: F is connected to G, X, K (all in S) and E. Node E is in T. Therefore, the edge **(F, E)** crosses the boundary from S to T.
    *   **From H**: H is connected to G (in S) and B, I, E. Nodes B, I, E are all in T. Therefore, the edges **(H, B)**, **(H, I)**, and **(H, E)** cross the boundary from S to T.

5.  **Form the Cut Set**: The complete set of edges that connect a node in S to a node in T is:
    **Cut Set = {(F, E), (H, B), (H, I), (H, E)}**

6.  **Verify Disconnection**: If these 4 edges are removed, there will be no path from any node in S (which includes G) to any node in T (which includes N). Specifically:
    *   Node F will be disconnected from E, thus cutting all paths from G through F to E and beyond.
    *   Node H will be disconnected from B, I, and E, thus cutting all paths from G through H to B, I, E and beyond.
    *   Since G is only connected to nodes within S (X, K, F, H), and all connections from F and H to T are severed, G becomes completely disconnected from N.

7.  **Determine Minimum Number of Edges**: The size of this cut set is 4. According to the Max-Flow Min-Cut theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect two nodes is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between them. Since I have found a valid cut of size 4, the minimum number of edges required is at most 4. I have also considered other potential cut sets (e.g., removing all edges directly connected to N, which also results in 4 edges: (N,B), (N,C), (N,O), (N,Y) or (N,B), (N,C), (N,O), (O,Y)), and they do not yield a smaller number. Therefore, 4 is the minimum.

The final answer is 4 edges.

1.  **Identify direct connections of target nodes:**
    *   Node M is directly connected to J, O, and L.
    *   Node B is directly connected to N, C, E, and O.

2.  **Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
    *   To isolate M, we can remove all edges connected to it: (M, J), (M, O), and (M, L). This would require removing 3 edges. After removing these, M would have no connections to the rest of the graph, thus no path to B.
    *   To isolate B, we can remove all edges connected to it: (B, N), (B, C), (B, E), and (B, O). This would require removing 4 edges.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut (1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    *   **Cut of 1 edge:** Removing any single edge (e.g., (M, O)) would still leave multiple paths between M and B (e.g., M-J-...-B, M-L-...-B). Therefore, a single edge is not sufficient.
    *   **Cut of 2 edges:** To determine if 2 edges are sufficient, we can try to find two "bottleneck" edges that all paths must traverse, or use Menger's Theorem by finding edge-disjoint paths. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then the minimum cut is at least 3.

4.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths between M and B:**

    *   **Path 1:** M - O - B

    *   Path 3 uses edges (M, L), (L, E), (E, B). These edges are not present in Path 1 or Path 2.

    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between M and B, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect M from B is at least 3.

5.  **Confirm the minimum cut:**
    We have identified a cut set of 3 edges by isolating M: [(M, J), (M, O), (M, L)]. Since we know the minimum cut must be at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is indeed the minimum. Removing these three edges ensures that M has no outgoing connections, thus no path to B.

    *   **Path 1: E - G - H**

    *   Node H is connected to: G, I, B.

2.  **Analyze the structure of paths to H:**
    All paths from E to H must eventually pass through one of H's direct neighbors: G, I, or B. Therefore, to disconnect H from the rest of the graph (and thus from E), we must remove all edges connecting H to its neighbors.

3.  **Propose a cut set based on H's direct connections:**
    The edges directly connected to H are (G, H), (I, H), and (B, H). If we remove these three edges, H becomes an isolated node, and no path can exist between E and H.
    This cut set is: `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]`.
    The size of this cut set is 3.

4.  **Verify if a smaller cut set (1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that it's impossible to disconnect E and H by removing only 1 or 2 edges. This can be done by finding a set of edge-disjoint paths between E and H. If we can find *k* edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least *k* edges must be removed to disconnect the two nodes.

    Let's identify edge-disjoint paths from E to H:
    *   **Path 1:** E - G - H
        Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    *   **Path 2:** E - B - H
        Edges: `(E, B)`, `(B, H)`
    *   **Path 3:** E - M - J - P - I - H
        Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`

5.  **Check for edge-disjointness:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges `(E, G)` and `(G, H)`.
    *   Path 2 uses edges `(E, B)` and `(B, H)`.
    *   Path 3 uses edges `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, P)`, `(P, I)`, `(I, H)`.
    These three paths do not share any common edges. For example, `(E, G)` is unique to Path 1, `(E, B)` to Path 2, and `(E, M)` to Path 3. Similarly, the edges connecting to H (`(G, H)`, `(B, H)`, `(I, H)`) are unique to each path.

6.  **Conclusion based on Menger's Theorem:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. Our proposed cut set `[(G, H), (I, H), (B, H)]` has exactly 3 edges and successfully disconnects E from H. Therefore, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are E and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from H. This is a classic min-cut problem, which can be solved by finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two target nodes (Max-Flow Min-Cut Theorem).

1.  **Identify direct neighbors of H:** H is connected to B, G, and I. Any path from E to H must pass through one of these three nodes and use the corresponding edge to reach H.

2.  **Search for edge-disjoint paths from E to H:**
    *   **Path 1:** E - B - H
        Edges: {(E, B), (B, H)}

    *   **Path 2:** Now, I need to find a path from E to H that does not use any of the edges from Path 1.
        From E, I can go to F. From F, I can go to G. From G, I can go to H.
        Path 2: E - F - G - H
        Edges: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
        Let's check for edge-disjointness with Path 1:
        {(E, B), (B, H)} and {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)} share no common edges. So, these two paths are edge-disjoint. This implies that at least 2 edges must be removed.

    *   **Path 3:** Now, I need to find a third path from E to H that does not use any of the edges from Path 1 or Path 2.
        Edges used so far: {(E, B), (B, H), (E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}.
        From E, the remaining unused edges are (E, L) and (E, M).
        To reach H, the only remaining unused edge directly connected to H is (I, H).
        So, I need to find a path from {L, M} to I using only unused edges.
        Let's try starting from E to M:
        E - M (edge (E, M) is available)
        From M, I can go to F. (M, F) is available.
        From F, (F, G) is used, (F, E) is used. Available from F: (F, K), (F, X).
        Let's choose F - K. (F, K) is available.
        From K, (K, G) is available, but (G, H) is used, so K-G-H is not edge-disjoint.
        Let's choose K - J. (K, J) is available.
        From J, (J, I) is available.
        From I, (I, H) is available.
        Path 3: E - M - F - K - J - I - H
        Edges: {(E, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)}
        Let's check for edge-disjointness with Path 1 and Path 2:
        Path 1: {(E, B), (B, H)}
        Path 2: {(E, F), (F, G), (G, H)}
        Path 3: {(E, M), (M, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, I), (I, H)}
        All edges in these three sets are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are edge-disjoint.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of edges:** Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between E and H, by the Max-Flow Min-Cut Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect E and H is at least 3.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** To achieve disconnection, I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal breaks all paths. A straightforward way to disconnect H from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to H.
    The edges connected to H are: (B, H), (G, H), and (I, H).
    If these three edges are removed, H becomes an isolated node, and no path can reach it from E (or any other node).

5.  **Verify the cut set:**
    *   Removing (B, H) breaks Path 1 (E-B-H) and any other path that uses (B, H).
    *   Removing (G, H) breaks Path 2 (E-F-G-H) and any other path that uses (G, H).
    *   Removing (I, H) breaks Path 3 (E-M-F-K-J-I-H) and any other path that uses (I, H).
    Since all paths to H must use one of these three edges as their final step, removing them guarantees complete disconnection.

This cut set has 3 edges, which matches the minimum number determined by the edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]

*   E -> B -> C -> O -> Y -> J -> D
*   E -> B -> N -> O -> P -> D
*   E -> B -> N -> O -> Y -> J -> D
    *   Node O is a critical point for all these paths. If O is removed, these paths are broken.

3.  **Remove O:** All paths from E that go through O (e.g., E-B-C-O-P-D, E-B-N-O-Y-J-D) are severed.

    *   J is connected to D, M, I, Y, P.
    *   M and I are removed.
    *   Y is connected to J and O. Since O is removed, Y is only connected to J. Can E reach Y? All paths from E to Y (E-B-C-O-Y, E-B-N-O-Y) require passing through O, which is removed. Thus, Y is disconnected from E.

    *   Therefore, J is disconnected from E.

*   **Can E reach P?**
    *   P is connected to D, I, O, J.
    *   I and O are removed.
    *   J is disconnected from E (as shown above).
    *   Therefore, P is disconnected from E.

**Is this the minimum number of nodes?**
Let's consider if a cut set of size 2 is possible.

*   If M is not removed, the path E-M-J-D remains.
*   If I is not removed, paths like E-B-H-I-J-D remain.
*   If O is not removed, paths like E-B-C-O-P-D remain.

    These two paths are edge-disjoint (they do not share any common edges).

    Now, let's try to find a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
    *   Edges used by Path 1 and Path 2: {(B, H), (H, E), (E, M), (B, C), (C, I), (I, J), (J, M)}.
    *   From B, the only remaining unused edge is (B, N). So, any third edge-disjoint path must start with (B, N).
    *   From M, the only remaining unused edge is (L, M). So, any third edge-disjoint path must end with (L, M).

    Let's try to construct a path from B to M using (B, N) and (L, M) and avoiding all edges from Path 1 and Path 2:
    *   Start from B: B - N
    *   From N, we can go to C or O.
        *   If N - C: (N, C) is available. From C, we can go to I or O. (C, I) is used by Path 2. So, we must go C - O.
        *   If N - O: (N, O) is available. From O, we can go to C, N, Y, P.
            *   If O - C: (O, C) is available. From C, we must go C - O (as C-I is used). This creates a cycle N-O-C-N, not leading to M.
            *   If O - P: (O, P) is available. From P, we can go to I, J, O, D.
                *   (P, I) is available. From I, (I, J) is used by Path 2. (I, P) is available (cycle).
                *   (P, J) is available. From J, (J, M) is used by Path 2.
                *   (P, D) is available. From D, (D, J) is available. From J, (J, M) is used by Path 2.

    It appears that any path starting with (B, N) will eventually encounter an edge already used by Path 2 (e.g., (J, M) or (I, J) or (P, J) if it goes through P).
    Similarly, any path trying to use (L, M) must come from E (B - ... - E - L - M). If it uses (H, E), it shares edges with Path 1. If it tries to reach E from G or F, it would need to find a path from B to G or F that is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2, which is not straightforward.

    This suggests that finding three *completely* edge-disjoint paths is difficult, and perhaps not possible.

5.  **Re-evaluate using the node cut concept:**
    By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove (edge connectivity) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths. Also, the minimum number of nodes to remove (node connectivity) is a lower bound for the edge connectivity.
    Let's find a minimum node cut. Consider the set of nodes {E, I, P}.
    *   If node E is removed, paths B-H-E-M and B-H-E-L-M are cut.
    *   If node I is removed, paths B-H-I-J-M, B-H-I-P-J-M, B-C-I-J-M, B-C-I-P-J-M, B-N-C-I-J-M, B-N-C-I-P-J-M are cut.
    *   If node P is removed, paths B-H-I-P-J-M, B-C-I-P-J-M, B-C-O-P-J-M, B-N-O-P-J-M, B-N-C-I-P-J-M are cut.

    If we remove nodes E, I, and P, all paths from B to M are broken. This is a node cut of size 3.
    Since the minimum node cut is 3, the minimum edge cut must be at least 3.

6.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have identified two different cut sets of 3 edges each (isolating B or isolating M), and we've established that the minimum cut must be at least 3, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.
    One such minimal cut set is to remove all edges directly connected to M.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [[['M', 'E'], ['M', 'J'], ['M', 'L']], [['M', 'J'], ['F', 'E'], ['B', 'E']]]}

    According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes to remove to disconnect N and P is equal to the maximum number of node-disjoint paths between N and P. Since I found 3 node-disjoint paths, the minimum cut set must contain at least 3 nodes.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:** To disconnect N and P, I must remove at least one node from each of these three node-disjoint paths. A straightforward approach is to remove the nodes closest to P in each path, or nodes that act as bottlenecks for multiple paths.

2.  **Explore Paths:** I will systematically identify paths from N to P. To determine the minimum number of nodes to remove, I will look for node-disjoint paths. If I can find *k* node-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, at least *k* nodes must be removed to disconnect the target nodes.

    Let's find node-disjoint paths (paths that share no intermediate nodes):
    *   **Path 1:** N - Y - O - P
        *   Nodes: {N, Y, O, P}
    *   **Path 2:** N - C - I - P
        *   Nodes: {N, C, I, P}
    *   **Path 3:** N - B - E - M - J - P
        *   Nodes: {N, B, E, M, J, P}

3.  **Verify Node-Disjoint Paths:**
    *   Path 1 uses intermediate nodes Y, O.
    *   Path 2 uses intermediate nodes C, I.
    *   Path 3 uses intermediate nodes B, E, M, J.
    All intermediate nodes {Y, O, C, I, B, E, M, J} are distinct. Therefore, these three paths are indeed node-disjoint.

4.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:** Since I have found 3 node-disjoint paths between N and P, the minimum number of nodes that must be removed to disconnect N and P (excluding N and P themselves) is at least 3.

5.  **Identify a Cut Set of Size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects N from P.
    Consider the set of nodes {O, I, M}.
    *   **Removing O:** This breaks Path 1 (N - Y - O - P). It also breaks any other path that uses O to reach P, such as N - C - O - P and N - B - C - O - P.
    *   **Removing I:** This breaks Path 2 (N - C - I - P). It also breaks any other path that uses I to reach P, such as N - B - C - I - P and N - B - H - I - P.
    *   **Removing M:** This breaks Path 3 (N - B - E - M - J - P). All paths that eventually reach P via J must pass through M (e.g., N - B - E - L - M - J - P, N - B - H - G - K - F - E - M - J - P, etc.). Therefore, removing M disconnects all paths to J.

6.  **Verify Disconnection:** After removing O, I, and M:
    *   All paths to P that went through O are broken.
    *   All paths to P that went through I are broken.
    *   All paths to P that went through J are broken (because M is removed, and M is a bottleneck for all paths to J).
    Since P can only be reached from N via O, I, or J, and all these routes are now severed, N and P are completely disconnected.

7.  **Conclusion:** A cut set of size 3 ([O, I, M]) has been found, and it has been established that a minimum of 3 nodes are required.

    If we remove all three of these edges, node N will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Consequently, no path will exist between H and N.
    This set of edges, [(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)], constitutes a cut set of size 3.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (e.g., size 1 or 2) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum number of edges, we can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths from H to N, then the minimum cut size must be at least 3.

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths from H to N:

    *   **Path 1 (P1): H - B - N**
        Edges used: `{(H, B), (B, N)}`

    *   **Path 2 (P2): H - I - C - N**
        Edges used: `{(H, I), (I, C), (C, N)}`

    *   **Path 3 (P3): H - G - E - L - M - J - P - O - Y - N**
        Edges used: `{(H, G), (G, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)}`

4.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    Let's list all edges used by these three paths and check for any overlaps:
    *   Edges in P1: (H, B), (B, N)
    *   Edges in P2: (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   Edges in P3: (H, G), (G, E), (E, L), (L, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

    Upon inspection, there are no common edges between any pair of these three paths. Therefore, these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

5.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths from H to N, by Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect H from N.
    We have also identified a cut set of 3 edges [(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)] that successfully disconnects H from N.
    Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient. The minimum cut set size is at least 2.

        *   Removing {E, B}: Paths through I still exist (e.g., H -> I -> C -> O).
        *   Removing {E, I}: Paths through B still exist (e.g., H -> B -> C -> O).
        *   Removing {B, I}: Paths through E still exist (e.g., H -> E -> M -> J -> P -> O).

        *   This set {C, N, P} also successfully disconnects H from O.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are G and I. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect G and I. I cannot remove G or I.

2.  **Explore all paths between G and I:** I will systematically identify distinct paths from G to I. To find the minimum cut set, it's helpful to identify vertex-disjoint paths, as Menger's Theorem states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

    Let's list three vertex-disjoint paths (excluding G and I themselves):

    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I
        *   Intermediate node: {H}

    *   **Path 2:** G - F - E - B - C - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {F, E, B, C}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 because none of {F, E, B, C} is H.

    *   **Path 3:** G - X - K - M - J - P - I
        *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, M, J, P}
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 1 because none of {X, K, M, J, P} is H.
        *   This path is vertex-disjoint from Path 2 because the sets {F, E, B, C} and {X, K, M, J, P} have no common nodes.

3.  **Determine the minimum number of nodes to remove:** Since I have found three vertex-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 nodes must be removed to disconnect G and I.

4.  **Find a cut set of size 3:** Now I need to find a set of 3 nodes whose removal disconnects G and I. I can choose one node from each of the identified vertex-disjoint paths.

    *   To break Path 1 (G - H - I), I must remove H.
    *   To break Path 2 (G - F - E - B - C - I), I can remove F, E, B, or C. Let's choose B.
    *   To break Path 3 (G - X - K - M - J - P - I), I can remove X, K, M, J, or P. Let's choose J.

    So, a potential cut set is [H, B, J].

5.  **Verify the chosen cut set:**
    *   **Remove H:** This immediately breaks Path 1 (G-H-I) and any other paths that rely on H as a bridge (e.g., G-H-B-C-I).
    *   **After removing H, consider remaining paths:**
        *   Paths from G (via X or F) to I (via C or P) that go through B (e.g., G-F-E-B-C-I).
        *   Paths from G (via X or F) to I (via J and P) (e.g., G-X-K-M-J-P-I).
    *   **Remove B:** This breaks all paths that use B to connect the left side of the graph (G, X, F, K, M, L, E) to the right side (C, N, O, P, I). For example, G-F-E-B-C-I is broken.
    *   **After removing H and B, consider remaining paths:** The only remaining "channel" for paths from G's side to I's side is through J (e.g., G-X-K-M-J-P-I).
    *   **Remove J:** This breaks all remaining paths that use J (e.g., G-X-K-M-J-P-I).

    With H, B, and J removed, there are no remaining paths from G to I. This confirms that [H, B, J] is a valid cut set of size 3.

Since the minimum number of nodes required is at least 3 (due to 3 vertex-disjoint paths), and I have found a cut set of size 3, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3.

    *   Node N is connected to: B, C, O, Y.

    *   This gives a cut set of size 4: `[(B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N)]`.
