    *   Let's assume a cut of size 3 is possible. This would mean that if we remove any 3 edges, there would still be a path between G and N.
    *   Consider the four edges directly connected to N: (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), (Y, N).
    *   If we remove any three of these four edges, one edge will always remain. For example:
        *   If we remove (B, N), (C, N), (O, N), the edge (Y, N) remains.

    *   Therefore, removing only 3 of the direct edges to N is not sufficient to disconnect G from N.

    *   To prevent paths from reaching B: We need to cut (H, B) and (E, B). (2 edges)
    *   To prevent paths from reaching C: We need to cut (I, C). (1 edge)
    *   To prevent paths from reaching O: We need to cut (P, O). (1 edge)

    *   Additionally, D is only connected to P and J. If P is removed, D becomes isolated from F's side of the graph, meaning no path from F can reach D, and thus no path can reach J via D.
    *   However, paths through M would still exist. For example, F -> G -> H -> I -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J.
    *   Therefore, removing {P, D} is not sufficient.

2.  **Consider removing nodes {D, M}:**
    *   If D is removed, paths through D are broken.
    *   If M is removed, paths through M are broken.
    *   However, paths through P would still exist. For example, F -> P -> J.
    *   Therefore, removing {D, M} is not sufficient.

3.  **Consider removing nodes {P, M}:**
    *   **Remove P:**
        *   All paths that go directly through P to J are broken (e.g., F -> P -> J).
        *   All paths that go through P to D and then to J are broken (e.g., F -> P -> D -> J). Since D is only connected to P and J, removing P effectively isolates D from F's side, meaning no path from F can reach J via D.
        *   All paths that go through P to I or O and then eventually to J are broken (e.g., F -> P -> I -> ... -> J, F -> P -> O -> ... -> J).
    *   **After removing P, the only remaining way to reach J from F is through M.** All such paths would look like F -> ... -> M -> J. Examples include:
        *   F -> G -> H -> I -> C -> B -> E -> M -> J
        *   F -> G -> H -> I -> C -> B -> E -> L -> M -> J
        *   F -> G -> H -> I -> C -> N -> O -> B -> E -> M -> J
        *   (and similar paths starting with F -> X -> K -> G -> ...)
    *   **Now, remove M:** All these remaining paths that go through M are also broken.

Since removing {P, M} breaks all paths to J (by directly removing P and M, and indirectly isolating D from F's side), this set of two nodes successfully disconnects F and J. As we determined that 1 node is not enough, 2 is the minimum.

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
1.  **Identify Target Nodes:** The two black nodes are N and H. Our goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect N from H.

2.  **Explore Paths and Identify Critical Connections:**
    I will look for all possible paths between N and H. To determine the minimum number of edges to remove, I can use Menger's Theorem, which states that the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between two nodes is equal to the minimum number of edges needed to disconnect them (the min-cut).

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from N to H:

    *   **Path 1:** N - C - H
        Edges: (N, C), (C, H)

    *   **Path 2:** N - B - H
        Edges: (N, B), (B, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 because it uses different edges (N,B) and (B,H) compared to (N,C) and (C,H).

    *   **Path 3:** N - O - P - I - H
        Edges: (N, O), (O, P), (P, I), (I, H)
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2. It uses (N,O) as its first edge, which is distinct from (N,C) and (N,B). The subsequent nodes (O, P, I) and edges are also distinct from those used in Path 1 and Path 2.

3.  **Determine Lower Bound for Cut Size:**
    Since I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between N and H, according to Menger's Theorem, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect N from H.

4.  **Find a Cut Set of Size 3:**
    Now I need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal will disconnect N from H. A straightforward approach is to consider all edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that lead towards the other target node.

    Node N has the following connections:
    *   (N, Y) - Y does not lead to H.
    *   (N, O) - O leads to H via P, I, F, G.
    *   (N, C) - C leads to H directly and via I, B.
    *   (N, B) - B leads to H directly and via C, E.

    If we remove the edges (N, O), (N, C), and (N, B), then N will be completely isolated from any path leading to H.
    *   Removing (N, O) breaks all paths starting with N-O-...
    *   Removing (N, C) breaks all paths starting with N-C-...
    *   Removing (N, B) breaks all paths starting with N-B-...

    Since all paths from N to H must begin with one of these three edges, removing them guarantees disconnection.

5.  **Verify Minimality:**
    We established that at least 3 edges are required (from the 3 edge-disjoint paths). We found a cut set of exactly 3 edges. Therefore, this is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(N, O), (N, C), (N, B)]

2.  **Analyze Connections from Target Nodes:**
    *   Node M is directly connected to L, E, and J.
    *   Node N is directly connected to B, C, O, and Y.

3.  **Identify All Possible Paths (or main branches of paths):**
    All paths from M to N must start by going through one of M's neighbors (L, E, J) and end by reaching one of N's neighbors (B, C, O, Y).

    I can categorize the paths into two main groups based on the first hop from M:
    *   **Group 1: Paths starting with M → E (or M → L → E)**
        These paths proceed from E to H, and then to B or C, which are connected to N.
        Examples:
        *   M - L - E - H - B - N
        *   M - L - E - H - C - N
        *   M - E - H - B - N
        *   M - E - H - C - N
        *   M - E - G - H - B - N
        *   M - E - G - H - C - N
        *   M - E - F - G - H - B - N
        *   M - E - F - G - H - C - N
        All paths in this group rely on node E to proceed towards N.

    *   **Group 2: Paths starting with M → J**
        These paths proceed from J to I or P, and then to C or O, which are connected to N.
        Examples:
        *   M - J - I - C - N
        *   M - J - I - P - O - N
        *   M - J - P - O - N
        *   M - J - P - I - C - N
        All paths in this group rely on node J to proceed towards N.

4.  **Evaluate Single Node Removal:**
    *   If I remove node **E**: All paths in Group 1 are broken. However, paths in Group 2 (e.g., M - J - I - C - N) still exist. So, removing E alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove node **J**: All paths in Group 2 are broken. However, paths in Group 1 (e.g., M - E - H - B - N) still exist. So, removing J alone is not enough.
    *   If I remove any other single node (L, H, I, P, B, C, O, G, F, K, X, D, Y), at least one path from each group would remain. For example, removing H would break all paths through H, but paths like M - J - I - C - N and M - J - P - O - N would still exist.

    Therefore, a single node removal is insufficient to disconnect M and N. The minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

5.  **Evaluate Two Node Removal:**
    Since all paths from M to N must pass through either the 'E' branch or the 'J' branch, removing both **E** and **J** should disconnect M from N.
    *   Removing **E** severs all connections from M that go through E (including those that first go through L then E).
    *   Removing **J** severs all connections from M that go through J.

    After removing E and J, M is only connected to L. Node L is connected to M and E. Since E is removed, L is now only connected to M. This means M is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from N.

6.  **Conclusion:** The minimum number of nodes to remove is 2, and the cut set is [E, J].

The final answer is:
Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, J]

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR3
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and N. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from N.

1.  **Identify all direct connections to the target nodes:**
    *   Node G is connected to E, F, H, K.
    *   Node N is connected to B, C, Y.

2.  **Consider the edges directly connected to N:**
    Any path from G to N must, as its final step, traverse one of the edges connected to N. These edges are (B, N), (C, N), and (Y, N). If we remove all three of these edges, node N will be completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus no path can exist between G and N. This forms a potential cut set of size 3: `[(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)]`.

3.  **Determine if a smaller cut set (1 or 2 edges) is possible:**
    To prove that 3 is the minimum, I need to show that it's impossible to disconnect G and N by removing only 1 or 2 edges. A common method for this is to find a number of edge-disjoint paths between the two target nodes. If there are `k` edge-disjoint paths, then at least `k` edges must be removed to disconnect the nodes (by Menger's Theorem).

    Let's find edge-disjoint paths from G to N:
    *   **Path 1:** G - E - B - N
        Edges: (G, E), (E, B), (B, N)
    *   **Path 2:** G - H - I - C - N
        Edges: (G, H), (H, I), (I, C), (C, N)
    *   **Path 3:** G - K - F - M - J - P - O - Y - N
        Edges: (G, K), (K, F), (F, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y), (Y, N)

    Let's verify that these three paths are indeed edge-disjoint:
    *   The first edges from G are (G, E), (G, H), and (G, K), which are distinct.
    *   The subsequent edges (E, B), (H, I), (K, F) are distinct and do not overlap with edges from other paths.
    *   The edges leading to N, (B, N), (C, N), and (Y, N), are also distinct.
    *   No other edges in these paths (e.g., (I, C), (F, M), (M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, Y)) are shared between any of the three paths.
    *   The nodes used in Path 1 are {G, E, B, N}.
    *   The nodes used in Path 2 are {G, H, I, C, N}.
    *   The nodes used in Path 3 are {G, K, F, M, J, P, O, Y, N}.
    *   Apart from the source (G) and sink (N), no intermediate nodes are shared between these three paths, which confirms they are node-disjoint and therefore also edge-disjoint.

4.  **Conclusion:**
    Since I have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths between G and N, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them. The cut set `[(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)]` successfully disconnects G from N by isolating N, and it consists of exactly 3 edges. Therefore, this is the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, N), (C, N), (Y, N)]

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and J. I need to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E and J. I cannot remove E or J.

First, I identify all possible paths between E and J. To do this systematically, I can consider the direct neighbors of E and J.

Neighbors of E: F, L, M, H, I
Neighbors of J: M, I, P, D

Any path from E to J must pass through one of the neighbors of E, and eventually reach one of the neighbors of J.

Let's identify potential "bottleneck" nodes. The nodes M, I, and P are crucial because they are the primary entry points to J from the rest of the graph (node D is only connected to J and P, so any path through D must also pass through P).

Consider the following three paths from E to J:
1.  **Path 1: E - M - J**
    *   This path uses node M.
2.  **Path 2: E - I - J**
    *   This path uses node I.
3.  **Path 3: E - H - B - C - P - J**
    *   This path uses nodes H, B, C, P.

Let's check if these three paths are node-disjoint, meaning they do not share any intermediate nodes (nodes other than E and J).
*   Intermediate nodes in Path 1: {M}
*   Intermediate nodes in Path 2: {I}
*   Intermediate nodes in Path 3: {H, B, C, P}

These sets of intermediate nodes are completely disjoint. Since I have found three node-disjoint paths between E and J, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect E and J is at least 3.

Now, I need to find a cut set of size 3.
As observed earlier, any path from E to J must pass through one of J's neighbors: M, I, P, or D.
*   If a path goes through M (e.g., E-M-J or E-L-M-J), removing M will break it.
*   If a path goes through I (e.g., E-I-J or E-H-I-J), removing I will break it.
*   If a path goes through P (e.g., E-H-B-C-P-J or E-H-B-N-O-P-J), removing P will break it.
*   If a path goes through D, it must be E -> ... -> P -> D -> J, because D is only connected to J and P. Thus, removing P would also break this path.

Therefore, removing the set of nodes {M, I, P} will disconnect E from J.
Since we established that the minimum cut size is at least 3, and we found a cut set of size 3, this is the minimum.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are F (black) and J (black). My goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to ensure no path exists between F and J.

First, I identify all direct connections to the target nodes:
- Node F is connected to E, X, K.
- Node J is connected to M, P, D.

I will systematically analyze paths and potential cut sets.

**1. Consider isolating one of the target nodes:**
To isolate node J, I would need to remove all edges directly connected to it: (J, M), (J, P), and (J, D). This would be a cut set of size 3.
To isolate node F, I would need to remove all edges directly connected to it: (F, E), (F, X), and (F, K). This would also be a cut set of size 3.
This establishes an upper bound for the minimum cut size at 3.

**2. Explore if a cut of size 1 or 2 is possible:**
Let's assume a cut of size 1 or 2 exists. This means there are one or two edges whose removal would disconnect F and J.

*   **Attempt to find a cut of size 1:**
    If I remove any single edge, there will always be multiple alternative paths. For example, removing (J, M) would still leave paths through (J, P) and (J, D). Removing (K, G) would still leave paths through E. Thus, a cut of size 1 is not possible.

*   **Attempt to find a cut of size 2:**
    Let's consider removing two edges. I will focus on the edges directly connected to J, as this simplifies the analysis of paths reaching J.
    *   **Scenario A: Remove (J, M) and (J, P).**
        After removing these two edges, J is only connected to D.
        Now, I need to check if any path from F can reach D, and then J via (D, J).
        Let's trace a path: F - K - G - H - B - C - P - D - J.
        Even though (J, P) is removed, the path F - ... - P - D - J is still active because P is connected to D, and D is connected to J.
        Therefore, removing (J, M) and (J, P) is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario B: Remove (J, M) and (J, D).**
        After removing these two edges, J is only connected to P.
        Let's trace a path: F - K - G - H - B - C - P - J.
        This path is still active.
        Therefore, removing (J, M) and (J, D) is not sufficient.

    *   **Scenario C: Remove (J, P) and (J, D).**
        After removing these two edges, J is only connected to M.
        Let's trace a path: F - E - M - J.
        This path is still active.
        Therefore, removing (J, P) and (J, D) is not sufficient.

    Since removing any two direct connections to J leaves at least one path to J active, a cut of size 2 is not possible by solely targeting J's direct connections. This strongly suggests that the minimum cut size is 3.

**3. Confirm a cut of size 3:**
As established in step 1, removing all edges directly connected to J will completely isolate J from the rest of the graph, thus disconnecting it from F.
The edges are: (J, M), (J, P), (J, D).

Let's verify this cut set:
*   **Path 1: F - E - M - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, M).
*   **Path 2: F - E - L - M - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, M).
*   **Path 3: F - X - K - G - H - B - N - Y - O - P - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, P).
*   **Path 4: F - X - K - G - H - B - C - P - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, P).
*   **Path 5: F - X - K - G - H - B - C - O - P - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, P).
*   **Path 6: F - X - K - G - H - B - N - C - P - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, P).
*   **Path 7: F - X - K - G - H - B - N - C - O - P - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, P).
*   **Path 8: F - X - K - G - H - B - C - P - D - J**
    This path is broken by removing (J, D).

All identified paths between F and J are broken by removing these three edges. Since we've shown that a cut of size 2 is not possible, 3 is the minimum.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The two black nodes are E and I. My goal is to find the minimum number of white nodes to remove to disconnect E from I. I cannot remove E or I.

I will systematically identify all paths from E to I and look for critical nodes that all paths must pass through.

First, let's list the direct neighbors of E and I:
Neighbors of E: H, G, M
Neighbors of I: H, J, P, C

Any path from E to I must start with one of E's neighbors and end with one of I's neighbors.

Let's categorize paths based on the first node from E:

**Group 1: Paths starting with E - H**
1.  E - H - I (Direct path)
2.  E - H - B - C - I
3.  E - H - B - N - C - I
4.  E - H - B - N - O - C - I
5.  E - H - B - N - O - P - I

**Group 2: Paths starting with E - G**
Let's examine the cluster connected to G: {G, F, K, X}.
Connections from this cluster to the rest of the graph (towards I):
-   G is connected to H.
-   F is connected to H.
There are no other connections from {G, F, K, X} to the rest of the graph that lead to I without passing through H.
Therefore, any path from E that goes through G (e.g., E-G-F-H-I, E-G-K-F-H-I, E-G-H-I) must eventually pass through node H to reach I.

**Group 3: Paths starting with E - M**
1.  E - M - J - I
2.  E - M - J - P - I
3.  E - M - J - P - O - C - I
4.  E - M - J - D - P - I
5.  E - M - J - D - P - O - C - I
6.  E - M - L - J - I (This path also uses J)
7.  E - M - L - J - P - I (This path also uses J and P)
And so on for paths through L-J.

**Identifying the Cut Set:**

From the analysis above:
-   All paths in Group 1 (starting E-H) pass through H.
-   All paths in Group 2 (starting E-G) must eventually pass through H to reach I.
    This means that if node H is removed, all paths from E that start with E-H or E-G are broken.

-   The remaining paths are those in Group 3, which start with E-M.
    All paths in Group 3 (starting E-M) must pass through M. After M, they all proceed through J (either directly E-M-J or E-M-L-J).
    Therefore, to break these remaining paths, we must remove either M or J.

So, to disconnect E and I, we need to remove node H (to break all paths through H and G) AND we need to remove either node M or node J (to break all paths through M).

This leads to two possible minimal cut sets of size 2:
1.  [H, M]: Removing H breaks all paths through H and G. Removing M breaks all paths through M.
2.  [H, J]: Removing H breaks all paths through H and G. Removing J breaks all paths through M (since all paths from M to I must pass through J).

Let's verify that a single node removal is not sufficient:
-   If H is removed: Paths like E-M-J-I still exist.
-   If M is removed: Paths like E-H-I still exist.
-   If J is removed: Paths like E-H-I still exist.
-   If any other single node is removed (e.g., G, F, P, C, B, N, O, K, X, L, D, Y), paths between E and I would still exist through H or M/J.

Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [H, M] or [H, J]

    *   Node P is connected to O, D, and J. If a path goes through P to J, it must have come from O or D. Since D itself comes from P, effectively, paths to P from E must come from O (E -> ... -> O -> P -> J).
    *   Node Y is connected to O and J. If a path goes through Y to J, it must have come from O (E -> ... -> O -> Y -> J).
    *   Node I is connected to H, C, N, and J. Paths to I can come from H, C, or N.

    *   Node I (E -> ... -> I -> J)
    *   Node P (E -> ... -> P -> J)
    *   Node Y (E -> ... -> Y -> J)

    Let's analyze the predecessors of P and Y:
    *   Any path to P (that doesn't originate from J itself via D) must pass through O (E -> ... -> O -> P -> J).
    *   Any path to Y must pass through O (E -> ... -> O -> Y -> J).

    Therefore, any path from E to J must either pass through I, or pass through O (to reach P or Y).
    This means that nodes I and O are critical for all paths from E to J. If both I and O are removed, no path can exist between E and J.

    *   **Remove I:** All paths that go E -> ... -> I -> J are broken. This includes paths from the "left" side of the graph (E -> M/F -> G -> H -> I -> J) and paths from the "right" side that converge on I (E -> B -> H -> I -> J, E -> B -> C -> I -> J, E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J). After removing I, the only remaining ways to reach J are via P or Y.
    *   **Remove O:** All paths that go E -> ... -> O -> P -> J or E -> ... -> O -> Y -> J are broken. Since P and Y are only connected to J via O (or D, which is connected to P), removing O severs all remaining paths to J.

    Thus, removing both I and O successfully disconnects E from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

5.  **Check for a smaller cut set (size 1):**
    I will systematically check if removing any single node can disconnect E and J.
    *   **M, L, K, X, F, G:** Removing any of these would still leave paths through B (e.g., E -> B -> C -> I -> J).

    *   **B:** Paths E -> M/F -> G -> H -> I -> J would still exist.

    *   **Y:** Paths E -> ... -> I -> J or E -> ... -> P -> J would still exist.

--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect the two black nodes, N and G. This is a minimum s-t cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between N and G.

1.  **Identify all direct neighbors of the target nodes:**
    *   Neighbors of N: Y, O, C, B
    *   Neighbors of G: H, K, F, X

2.  **Explore paths from N to G and identify edge-disjoint paths:**
    Let's try to find as many edge-disjoint paths as possible.

    *   **Path 1:** N - O - P - F - G
        Edges used: {(N,O), (O,P), (P,F), (F,G)}

    *   **Path 2:** N - C - H - G
        Edges used: {(N,C), (C,H), (H,G)}

    These two paths (Path 1 and Path 2) are edge-disjoint, meaning they do not share any common edges. This implies that the minimum number of edges to remove is at least 2.

3.  **Attempt to find a third edge-disjoint path:**
    To prove that the minimum cut is 2, we must show that no third edge-disjoint path exists. Let's try to construct one using only the edges not used in Path 1 or Path 2.

    *   **Edges used so far:**
        *   From Path 1: (N,O), (O,P), (P,F), (F,G)
        *   From Path 2: (N,C), (C,H), (H,G)

    *   **Remaining available edges from N:** (N,B), (N,Y)
        *   (N,Y) leads to a dead end (Y is only connected to N). So, any third path must start with (N,B).

    *   **Starting with N - B:**
        *   **Option A: N - B - E - ... - G**
            *   From E, we can go to P, L, M.
            *   If we go N - B - E - P - ... - G:
                *   (P,F) is already used in Path 1.
                *   (P,O) is already used in Path 1.
                *   From P, we can go to I or J.
                *   If N - B - E - P - I - ... - G:
                    *   (I,H) is available, but (H,G) is used in Path 2. So, N - B - E - P - I - H - G is not edge-disjoint.
                    *   (I,C) is available, but (C,H) and (H,G) are used in Path 2. So, N - B - E - P - I - C - H - G is not edge-disjoint.
                *   If N - B - E - P - J - ... - G: J is connected to D, M, I. None of these can reach G without using already used edges or leading to dead ends/cycles.
            *   If N - B - E - L - M - E (cycle) - no path to G.
            *   If N - B - E - M - J - ... - G (similar to above, no path to G).

        *   **Option B: N - B - C - ... - G**
            *   (C,H) is already used in Path 2.
            *   From C, we can go to I or O.
            *   If N - B - C - I - ... - G:
                *   (I,H) is available, but (H,G) is used in Path 2. So, N - B - C - I - H - G is not edge-disjoint.
                *   (I,P) is available, but (P,F) and (F,G) are used in Path 1. So, N - B - C - I - P - F - G is not edge-disjoint.
            *   If N - B - C - O - N (cycle) - no path to G.

    *   **Consider paths to G via K or X:**
        *   K is connected to G, F, X.
        *   X is connected to G, K, F.
        *   To reach K or X from N, one must first reach F (since K and X are only connected to F, G, or each other).
        *   To reach F from N, one must use the edge (P,F) (as P is the only node connecting to F from the N-side of the graph).
        *   However, (P,F) is already used in Path 1. Therefore, no path to G via K or X can be edge-disjoint from Path 1.

    Since all attempts to find a third edge-disjoint path fail, we conclude that there are at most 2 edge-disjoint paths between N and G. By Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

4.  **Identify a minimal cut set of size 2:**
    Based on the two edge-disjoint paths identified:
    *   Path 1: N - O - P - F - G
    *   Path 2: N - C - H - G

    We can choose one edge from each path such that their removal disconnects N and G.
    Consider the edges (F,G) from Path 1 and (H,G) from Path 2.

    *   **Cut Set: [(F,G), (H,G)]**
        *   **Removal of (F,G):** This edge directly connects F to G. Any path from N to G that passes through F is broken. Furthermore, K and X are only connected to G and F (and each other). If F cannot reach G (due to (F,G) being removed), then any path N-...-F-K-G or N-...-F-X-G is also broken. Since K and X cannot be reached from N without passing through F, removing (F,G) effectively disconnects all paths to G that would pass through F, K, or X.
        *   **Removal of (H,G):** This edge directly connects H to G. Any path from N to G that passes through H is broken.

    Since all paths from N to G must eventually pass through H, F, K, or X, and the removal of (F,G) and (H,G) breaks all these routes, N and G are completely disconnected.

The minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Image name: Tu_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B (black) and M (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect B and M.

1.  **Identify the target nodes and their direct connections:**
    *   Node B is connected to: C, N, O, E, H, I.
    *   Node M is connected to: E, L, J.

2.  **Consider a simple strategy: Isolate one of the target nodes.**
    *   To isolate M, we can remove all edges directly connected to it: (E, M), (L, M), and (J, M).
    *   If these three edges are removed, M will have no connections to the rest of the graph, thus ensuring no path exists between B and M.
    *   This gives a potential cut set of size 3: `[(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]`.

3.  **Check if a smaller cut (e.g., 2 edges) is possible.**
    *   To prove that 3 is the minimum, we need to show that there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M. If we can find 3 edge-disjoint paths, then by Menger's Theorem, the minimum edge cut must be at least 3.

4.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths from B to M:**
    *   **Path 1:** B - E - M
        *   Edges: `(B, E)`, `(E, M)`
    *   **Path 2:** B - C - I - P - J - M
        *   Edges: `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, J)`, `(J, M)`
    *   **Path 3:** B - N - O - P - E - L - M
        *   Edges: `(B, N)`, `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`

5.  **Verify edge-disjointness of the identified paths:**
    *   Path 1 uses edges `(B, E)` and `(E, M)`.
    *   Path 2 uses edges `(B, C)`, `(C, I)`, `(I, P)`, `(P, J)`, `(J, M)`. None of these edges are in Path 1.
    *   Path 3 uses edges `(B, N)`, `(N, O)`, `(O, P)`, `(P, E)`, `(E, L)`, `(L, M)`. None of these edges are in Path 1 or Path 2.
    *   All three paths are indeed edge-disjoint.

6.  **Conclusion on minimum cut size:**
    *   Since there are 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and M, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect them.
    *   The cut set `[(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]` successfully disconnects M from the rest of the graph (and thus from B) by removing 3 edges.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

7.  **Final check of the chosen cut set:**
    *   If `(E, M)` is removed, Path 1 (B-E-M) is broken.
    *   If `(L, M)` is removed, Path 3 (B-N-O-P-E-L-M) is broken.
    *   If `(J, M)` is removed, Path 2 (B-C-I-P-J-M) is broken.
    *   All three identified edge-disjoint paths are broken by this cut set.
    *   Any other path to M would have to use one of these three edges as its final step to M, or pass through E, L, or J. By removing all edges directly connected to M, M is completely isolated.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(E, M), (L, M), (J, M)]

Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, M and B. This is a minimum cut problem. According to Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges in an edge cut is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The black nodes are M and B.

2.  **Identify edges connected to M and B:**
    *   Edges connected to M: (M, L), (M, E), (M, J)
    *   Edges connected to B: (E, B), (H, B), (C, B), (N, B)

3.  **Attempt to find edge-disjoint paths from M to B:**
    Let's try to find paths that do not share any common edges.

    *   **Path 1 (P1): M - E - B**
        Edges used: {(M, E), (E, B)}

    *   Now, let's try to find a second path (P2) that is edge-disjoint from P1.
        *   Consider paths starting from M via L: M - L - E - B. This path uses the edge (E, B), which is already used by P1. Therefore, M - L - E - B cannot be edge-disjoint from P1. This implies that any path from M that goes through L or E must eventually use the edge (E, B) to reach B. Thus, only one edge-disjoint path can be formed through the "E-branch" (M-E or M-L-E).
        *   Consider paths starting from M via J:
            *   **Path 2 (P2): M - J - I - H - B**
                Edges used: {(M, J), (J, I), (I, H), (H, B)}
                Let's check if P2 is edge-disjoint from P1:
                - Edges of P1: (M, E), (E, B)
                - Edges of P2: (M, J), (J, I), (I, H), (H, B)
                These two sets of edges are completely distinct. So, P1 and P2 are edge-disjoint.

    *   Now, let's try to find a third path (P3) that is edge-disjoint from both P1 and P2.
        *   As established, no other path from the "E-branch" (M-L-E) can be edge-disjoint from P1 because it would share (E, B).
        *   Therefore, P3 must originate from M via J.
        *   Let's consider another path from M via J: M - J - P - O - C - B.
            Edges used: {(M, J), (J, P), (P, O), (O, C), (C, B)}
            Let's check if this path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2:
            - It is edge-disjoint from P1 (no common edges).
            - However, it shares the edge (M, J) with P2. Therefore, this path is NOT edge-disjoint from P2.

    *   Since all paths from M must either go through E (and thus use (E,B) if P1 uses it) or through J (and thus use (M,J) if P2 uses it), we cannot find a third path that is mutually edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.

4.  **Conclusion on maximum edge-disjoint paths:**
    We have found 2 edge-disjoint paths (P1 and P2). We have also shown that it's not possible to find 3 mutually edge-disjoint paths. Therefore, the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between M and B is 2.

5.  **Determine the minimum cut:**
    By Menger's theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect M and B is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths, which is 2.

6.  **Identify a cut set of 2 edges:**
    To disconnect M and B, we need to remove edges that cut all paths. Based on the two edge-disjoint paths identified:
    *   P1: M - E - B
    *   P2: M - J - I - H - B

    We can choose one edge from each of these paths such that their removal disconnects M and B.
    Let's choose (E, B) from P1 and (M, J) from P2.
    *   Removing (E, B) disconnects M from B via the "E-branch" (M-E-B and M-L-E-B).
    *   Removing (M, J) disconnects M from all paths that start from M and go through J (e.g., M-J-I-H-B, M-J-P-O-C-B, M-J-P-O-N-B, etc.).

    With both (E, B) and (M, J) removed, M has no path to B.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(E, B), (M, J)]

        *   Edges: {(E,M), (M,J)}
    *   **Path B:** E - I - J
        *   Edges: {(E,I), (I,J)}

    *   Edges in Path C: (E,B), (B,C), (C,O), (O,P), (P,J)

    None of the edges in Path B appear in Path A or C.
    None of the edges in Path C appear in Path A or B.
    Therefore, these three paths (A, B, C) are indeed edge-disjoint.

    According to Menger's Theorem, since there are at least 3 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and J must be at least 3.

Image name: POGBb_task3_gt3_PAIR1
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are B and H. My goal is to find the minimum number of edges that must be removed to completely disconnect B from H. I will use the concept of edge-disjoint paths, as the minimum number of edges to remove (minimum cut) is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two target nodes (Menger's Theorem).

1.  **Identify the target nodes:** The two black nodes are B and H.

2.  **Find edge-disjoint paths between B and H:**
    *   **Path 1:** A direct connection exists:
        B - H
        Edges: `{(B, H)}`

    *   **Path 2:** Let's look for a path that does not use the edge (B, H).
        B - C - I - H
        Edges: `{(B, C), (C, I), (I, H)}`
        This path is edge-disjoint from Path 1, as it uses different edges.

    *   **Path 3:** Let's look for a third path that is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.
        B - N - E - F - X - G - H
        Edges: `{(B, N), (N, E), (E, F), (F, X), (X, G), (G, H)}`
        Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from Path 1 and Path 2:
        -   It does not use (B, H) (Path 1).
        -   It does not use (B, C), (C, I), or (I, H) (Path 2).
        Therefore, Path 3 is edge-disjoint from both Path 1 and Path 2.

3.  **Determine the minimum cut size:**
    I have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between B and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect B and H is at least 3.

4.  **Propose a cut set of size 3 and verify:**
    To disconnect H from B, all paths leading to H must be severed. The node H has three direct connections: to B, G, and I. Any path from B to H must eventually use one of these three edges to reach H.
    Consider the cut set: `[(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]`.
    *   Removing `(B, H)` breaks the direct path (Path 1).
    *   Removing `(G, H)` breaks all paths that enter H via node G (e.g., Path 3: B-N-E-F-X-G-H).
    *   Removing `(I, H)` breaks all paths that enter H via node I (e.g., Path 2: B-C-I-H).

    After removing these three edges, node H is completely isolated from the rest of the graph, and thus from node B. Since we found 3 edge-disjoint paths, and we found a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects B and H, this is the minimum number of edges.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 3, Cut Set: [(B, H), (G, H), (I, H)]
--- Ground Truth ---
{'length': 3, 'cut_set': [['B', 'H'], ['H', 'I'], ['G', 'H']]}

Image name: FD-K_task3_gt3_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal

Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect the two black nodes, E and H. This is a minimum cut problem, which by Menger's Theorem, is equal to the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify as many edge-disjoint paths as possible between E and H.

1.  **Path 1 (P1):** E - H
    Edges: `(E, H)`

2.  **Path 2 (P2):** E - F - H
    Edges: `(E, F)`, `(F, H)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from P1.

3.  **Path 3 (P3):** E - G - H
    Edges: `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`
    This path is edge-disjoint from P1 and P2.

4.  **Path 4 (P4):** E - M - J - I - H
    Edges: `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`
    Let's check if these edges are disjoint from P1, P2, P3:
    *   `(E, M)` is not in P1, P2, P3.
    *   `(M, J)` is not in P1, P2, P3.
    *   `(J, I)` is not in P1, P2, P3.
    *   `(I, H)` is not in P1, P2, P3.
    So, P4 is edge-disjoint from P1, P2, P3.

Now, let's try to find a fifth edge-disjoint path.
Edges used so far: `(E, H)`, `(E, F)`, `(F, H)`, `(E, G)`, `(G, H)`, `(E, M)`, `(M, J)`, `(J, I)`, `(I, H)`.

Consider the remaining edges connected to E: `(E, L)`.
Consider the remaining edges connected to H: `(H, B)`.

Let's try to construct a path using `(E, L)` and `(H, B)`:
*   Start from E: `E - L`.
*   From L: `L - M`. (Edge `(L, M)` is not used).
*   From M: `M - J`. (Edge `(M, J)` is already used by P4).
    Since `(M, J)` is used, any path from E-L-M cannot proceed to J without reusing an edge. M is only connected to L and J. Therefore, no path from E-L-M can reach H without using an already used edge.

This indicates that there are a maximum of 4 edge-disjoint paths between E and H. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges to remove to disconnect E and H is 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that actually disconnects E and H. A valid cut set must contain at least one edge from each of the 4 edge-disjoint paths identified.

Let's consider the following cut set: `[(E, H), (E, F), (G, H), (M, J)]`

1.  **Remove (E, H):** This cuts P1 (E-H).
2.  **Remove (E, F):** This cuts P2 (E-F-H). It also cuts any other path starting with E-F.
3.  **Remove (G, H):** This cuts P3 (E-G-H). It also cuts any other path ending with G-H.
4.  **Remove (M, J):** This cuts P4 (E-M-J-I-H). It also cuts any other path that passes through M-J.

Let's verify that no path remains between E and H after removing these 4 edges:
*   **Direct path:** `E-H` is cut by `(E, H)`.
*   **Paths through F:** `E-F-H` is cut by `(E, F)`. Any path `E-F-...-H` is cut by `(E, F)`.
*   **Paths through G:** `E-G-H` is cut by `(G, H)`. Any path `E-...-G-H` is cut by `(G, H)`.
    (Note: Paths like `E-F-G-H` are cut by `(E,F)` or `(G,H)`. Paths like `E-G-F-H` are cut by `(E,G)` or `(F,H)` - but `(E,G)` and `(F,H)` are not in the cut set. However, `(E,F)` and `(G,H)` are in the cut set, so `E-F-G-H` is cut by `(E,F)` and `E-G-F-H` is cut by `(G,H)`.)
    After removing `(E,F)` and `(G,H)`, E cannot reach F, and H cannot be reached from G. This effectively disconnects E from H via the F-G cluster.

*   **Paths through M-J:**
    *   `E-M-J-I-H` is cut by `(M, J)`.
    *   `E-M-J-P-I-H` is cut by `(M, J)`.
    *   `E-M-J-P-C-B-H` is cut by `(M, J)`.
    *   Any path starting `E-M-...` that goes through `J` is cut by `(M, J)`.

*   **Paths through L:**
    *   `E-L-M-J-I-H`: This path uses `(M, J)`, which is cut. So, this path is broken.
    *   `E-L-M-J-P-I-H`: This path uses `(M, J)`, which is cut. So, this path is broken.
    *   `E-L-M-J-P-C-B-H`: This path uses `(M, J)`, which is cut. So, this path is broken.
    Since L is only connected to M, and M's only path to the rest of the graph (towards H) is through J, cutting `(M, J)` effectively isolates E-L-M from H.

All identified paths and potential alternative paths between E and H are broken by this set of 4 edges.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(E, H), (E, F), (G, H), (M, J)]

    *   Node J is connected to M, P, and D.

    *   Path 1: L - M - J
    *   Path 2: L - E - P - J
    *   Path 3: L - E - M - J
    *   Path 4: L - E - P - D - J
    *   Path 5: L - E - B - C - I - P - J (and many other longer paths that eventually lead to J through M, P, or D)

    *   If we remove (L, E), L is still connected to M, and the path L-M-J still exists.
    *   If we remove (L, M), L is still connected to E, and paths like L-E-P-J, L-E-M-J, L-E-P-D-J still exist.
    *   If we remove any other single edge (e.g., (M, J), (P, J), (D, J), (E, P), etc.), there will always be at least one alternative path remaining between L and J. For example, removing (M, J) leaves L-E-P-J and L-E-P-D-J.
    Therefore, a single edge removal is not sufficient to disconnect L and J. The minimum number of edges must be at least 2.

4.  **Search for a cut of size 2:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all its incident edges.
    *   Consider node L. Its incident edges are (L, E) and (L, M).
    *   If we remove both (L, E) and (L, M):
        *   All paths starting with (L, E) are broken.
        *   All paths starting with (L, M) are broken.
        *   Since L has no other connections, it becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph.
        *   Consequently, no path can exist between L and J.

    This set of edges, [(L, E), (L, M)], successfully disconnects L from J and has a size of 2.

5.  **Verify minimality:**
    Since we established that a cut of size 1 is not possible, and we found a valid cut of size 2, this must be the minimum number of edges required.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(L, E), (L, M)]

        *   E - M - F - G - K - J
        *   (Note: L and M are only connected to E and F (via M-F) or each other. So, any path from E through L or M must eventually pass through F to reach J's side of the graph.)
    *   **Paths starting E -> F:**
        *   E - F - K - J
        *   E - F - G - K - J
        *   E - F - X - G - K - J
        *   (These paths directly use F to reach K, a neighbor of J.)

        *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
        *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
        *   (These paths use B to reach K, I, or P, which are neighbors of J.)

    *   Notice that all paths from E that go towards K (a neighbor of J) must pass through F or G or H.
    *   Notice that all paths from E that go towards I or P (neighbors of J) must pass through B.

5.  **Test for a cut set of size 1:**
    *   If I remove F: Paths through B (e.g., E-B-H-I-J) still exist.
    *   If I remove B: Paths through F (e.g., E-F-K-J) still exist.
    *   If I remove K, I, or P: Other neighbors of J still provide paths.
    *   If I remove G, H, C, N, O, X, L, M, Y: In each case, multiple alternative paths remain.
    *   Therefore, a cut set of size 1 is not possible. The minimum number of nodes to remove is at least 2.

6.  **Test for a cut set of size 2:**
    *   Consider the two main "channels" from E to J:
        *   **Channel 1 (Upper/Left):** Paths that primarily use F, G, X, K to reach J.
        *   **Channel 2 (Lower/Right):** Paths that primarily use B, H, C, N, Y, O, I, P to reach J.

        *   If F is removed, all paths that directly use F (E-F-K-J, E-F-G-K-J, E-F-X-G-K-J) are broken.

            *   E - B - H - G - K - J
            *   E - B - H - I - J
            *   E - B - C - I - J
            *   E - B - N - C - I - J
            *   E - B - C - O - P - J
            *   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
            *   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
        *   All these remaining paths pass through node B.

    *   Now, if I also remove node **B**:
        *   All the remaining paths (listed above) are broken because they all require node B.
        *   Since removing F broke all paths through the "upper" channel, and removing B broke all paths through the "lower" channel, E is now completely disconnected from J.

    *   Therefore, the set {F, B} is a valid cut set of size 2.

Image name: SEIS_task3_gt2_PAIR2
Task: task3_min_edges_removal
Model: gemini-2.5-flash
Mode: few_shot, COT: cot, Attempt: 13
--- Model Response ---
Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are G and I. My task is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect G from I. This is a classic min-cut problem, which, by Menger's Theorem, is equivalent to finding the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths between the two nodes.

First, I will identify a set of edge-disjoint paths between G and I. Edge-disjoint means that no two paths share any common edges.

1.  **Path 1: G - H - I**
    Edges: (G, H), (H, I)

2.  **Path 2: G - B - C - I**
    Edges: (G, B), (B, C), (C, I)
    (This path does not share any edges with Path 1).

3.  **Path 3: G - E - M - J - I**
    Edges: (G, E), (E, M), (M, J), (J, I)
    (This path does not share any edges with Path 1 or Path 2).

4.  **Path 4: G - F - K - J - P - I**
    Edges: (G, F), (F, K), (K, J), (J, P), (P, I)
    Let's verify if this path is edge-disjoint from the previous three:
    *   Compared to Path 1 (G-H-I): No common edges.
    *   Compared to Path 2 (G-B-C-I): No common edges.
    *   Compared to Path 3 (G-E-M-J-I): Path 3 uses (J, I), while Path 4 uses (J, P) and (P, I). These are distinct edges. So, no common edges.

Since I have found 4 edge-disjoint paths between G and I, according to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect G and I is 4.

Next, I need to identify a cut set of 4 edges that achieves this disconnection. A straightforward way to disconnect a node from the rest of the graph is to remove all edges directly connected to it. Node I has four direct connections: (H, I), (J, I), (C, I), and (P, I).

Let's verify that removing these four edges disconnects G from I:
If the edges (H, I), (J, I), (C, I), and (P, I) are removed, node I becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Therefore, no path can exist from G to I. This set of edges successfully disconnects the two black nodes.

This cut set has a size of 4, which matches the minimum number determined by the edge-disjoint paths.

Final Answer:
Edges to remove: 4, Cut Set: [(H, I), (J, I), (C, I), (P, I)]

    *   To disconnect O, we would need to remove all its neighbors (C, N, P, Y), which is 4 nodes.
    *   Therefore, a single node removal is not sufficient.

I will try to find vertex-disjoint paths between H and O (paths that do not share any intermediate nodes).
    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N - O
        *   Intermediate nodes: {B, N}

    *   **Path 3:** H - G - F - E - M - J - P - O

    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 2 is {I, C}.
    *   The set of intermediate nodes for Path 3 is {G, F, E, M, J, P}.
    *   These three sets are mutually disjoint. This confirms that there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and O.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 3.

6.  **Find a cut set of size 3:**
    *   Consider the direct neighbors of O: C, N, P, Y.
    *   If we remove C, N, and P, then O is only connected to Y.
    *   Now, I need to check if there is any path from H to Y.
    *   H's neighbors are G, F, B, I. None of these nodes, nor any nodes reachable from them (without passing through C, N, P), connect to Y. Y is only connected to O and N. Since N is removed, Y is only connected to O.
    *   Therefore, removing C, N, and P will completely disconnect H from O.

7.  **Conclusion:** Since I found 3 vertex-disjoint paths, the minimum cut size is at least 3. I also found a cut set of size 3 ([C, N, P]) that successfully disconnects H from O. Thus, 3 is the minimum number of nodes to remove.

        *   Node N is the target node!

1.  **Identify direct connection:** Nodes H and B are directly connected by the edge (H, B). Any path from H to B can use this direct connection. Therefore, to disconnect H from B, the edge (H, B) must be removed, or all alternative paths must be cut.

2.  **Identify alternative paths:** If the direct edge (H, B) is removed, I need to find all other paths from H to B.
    *   **Paths through G:** H is connected to G. G is connected to B.
        *   Path 1: H - G - B
        *   Path 2: H - G - F - E - B
    *   **Paths through I:** H is connected to I. I can lead to B through several routes.
        *   Path 3: H - I - C - B
        *   Path 4: H - I - P - O - C - B
        *   Path 5: H - I - P - J - M - E - B
        *   Path 6: H - I - P - J - M - L - E - B

3.  **Analyze minimum cut size:**
    *   **Can it be 1 edge?** No, because there are multiple distinct paths (e.g., H-B, H-G-B, H-I-C-B). Removing any single edge would leave other paths intact.
    *   **Can it be 2 edges?** Let's assume we remove two edges, e1 and e2.
        *   If e1 = (H, B), then e2 must cut all remaining paths (those through G and those through I).
            *   The paths through G (H-G-B, H-G-F-E-B) form one "bundle" originating from H.
            *   The paths through I (H-I-C-B, H-I-P-O-C-B, H-I-P-J-M-E-B, H-I-P-J-M-L-E-B) form another "bundle" originating from H.
            *   These two bundles diverge at H (one goes to G, the other to I). There is no single edge that can cut *all* paths in both the G-bundle and the I-bundle simultaneously. For example, if e2 = (H, G), it cuts the G-bundle, but the I-bundle remains. If e2 = (H, I), it cuts the I-bundle, but the G-bundle remains. If e2 is an edge further away (like (G, B), (C, B), or (E, B)), it only cuts some paths within a bundle or only one bundle, leaving others. Therefore, two edges are not sufficient if (H, B) is one of them.
        *   If neither e1 nor e2 is (H, B), then the direct path H-B remains, so H and B are still connected.
        *   Thus, a cut of size 2 is not possible.

4.  **Determine a minimal cut set of 3 edges:**
    Since 2 edges are not enough, the minimum must be at least 3.
    Consider the three "entry points" from H to the rest of the graph that lead to B:
    *   The direct connection (H, B).
    *   The connection through G via (H, G).
    *   The connection through I via (H, I).

    To disconnect H from B, we must cut all these "channels". The most straightforward way to do this is to remove the edges directly connected to H that lead to B:
    *   Remove (H, B) to cut the direct path.
    *   Remove (H, G) to cut all paths starting H-G.
    *   Remove (H, I) to cut all paths starting H-I.

    By removing these three edges, H becomes completely isolated from G, I, and B, ensuring no path exists between H and B.

    *   Edges to remove: [(H, B), (H, G), (H, I)]
    *   After removing (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I), node H has no outgoing edges. Therefore, no path can originate from H to reach B. This set successfully disconnects H from B.
    *   As established in step 3, a smaller set of edges (1 or 2) cannot achieve this disconnection.

7.  **E - B - C - O - P - J**
8.  **E - B - N - C - O - P - J**
9.  **E - B - N - Y - O - P - J**

Now, let's analyze the "bottleneck" nodes for these paths. I can group the paths based on the nodes directly connected to J (M, I, P, D). Node D is only connected to J and has no incoming paths from E, so it's not relevant for disconnecting E from J. Thus, we need to disconnect E from M, E from I, and E from P.

**Group 1: Paths to J via M**
*   E - M - J
*   E - L - M - J
    To break all paths in this group, node M must be removed. (Removing L would still leave E-M-J).

**Group 2: Paths to J via I**
*   E - F - K - G - H - I - J
*   E - F - X - K - G - H - I - J
*   E - B - H - I - J
*   E - B - C - I - J
    All these paths eventually reach I. To break all paths in this group, node I must be removed. (Alternatively, one could remove F and B, or H and C, but removing I is a single node solution for this group).

**Group 3: Paths to J via P**
*   E - B - C - O - P - J
*   E - B - N - C - O - P - J
*   E - B - N - Y - O - P - J
    All these paths eventually reach P. To break all paths in this group, node P must be removed. (Alternatively, one could remove B, or O, or C and N, but removing P is a single node solution for this group).

Based on this analysis, to disconnect E from J, we need to ensure that no paths remain through M, I, or P.
Therefore, a cut set consisting of [M, I, P] would disconnect E from J.
Let's verify this:
*   Removing M breaks paths 1 and 2.
*   Removing I breaks paths 3, 4, 5, and 6.
*   Removing P breaks paths 7, 8, and 9.
All paths are broken. This cut set has a size of 3.

Now, I need to confirm that this is the *minimum* number of nodes.
*   **Can it be done with 1 node?** No single node appears in all paths. For example, removing M leaves paths through I and P. Removing I leaves paths through M and P. Removing P leaves paths through M and I.
*   **Can it be done with 2 nodes?**
    *   If we remove [M, I]: Paths through P (e.g., E - B - C - O - P - J) still exist.
    *   If we remove [M, P]: Paths through I (e.g., E - F - K - G - H - I - J) still exist.
    *   If we remove [I, P]: Paths through M (e.g., E - M - J) still exist.
    *   Consider other combinations of 2 nodes, for example, [F, B]. This would cut all paths that go through F or B. However, paths E - M - J and E - L - M - J would still exist.
    *   Consider [M, B]. This would cut paths through M and paths through B. However, paths through F (e.g., E - F - K - G - H - I - J) would still exist.
    *   Consider [M, F]. This would cut paths through M and paths through F. However, paths through B (e.g., E - B - H - I - J) would still exist.

Since no set of 1 or 2 nodes can disconnect E and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove is 3. The cut set [M, I, P] is one such minimal set. Another valid minimal cut set would be [M, F, B], as removing M cuts the M-branch, removing F cuts the F-branch (to I), and removing B cuts the B-branch (to I and P).

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 3, Cut Set: [M, I, P]

{'length': 3, 'cut_set': ['F', 'B', 'M']}

4.  **Attempt to find a cut set of size 2:**

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - E
        Edges: (H, B), (B, E)

    *   Path 2 uses (H, G), (G, F), and (F, E).

5.  **Apply Menger's Theorem:**
    Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths between H and E, by Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect H and E (the minimum edge cut) must be at least 3.

6.  **Identify a cut set of size 3:**
    A straightforward way to disconnect H from the rest of the graph (and thus from E) is to remove all edges directly connected to H. These are (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I).
    *   If we remove (H, B), (H, G), and (H, I), node H becomes isolated. No path can originate from H to reach E.
    *   This cut set has 3 edges.

7.  **Conclusion:**
    Since we have identified 3 edge-disjoint paths (meaning the minimum cut is at least 3), and we have found a cut set of 3 edges that successfully disconnects H from E, the minimum number of edges to remove is 3.

Step-by-step reasoning.
The target nodes are E and J. The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect E from J.

1.  **Identify all possible paths between E and J.**
    I will look for distinct "channels" or "bundles" of paths that connect E to J.

    *   **Channel 1 (via M):**
        *   Path 1.1: E - M - J
        *   Path 1.2: E - L - M - J
        *   Path 1.3: E - F - G - X - K - M - J

    *   **Channel 2 (via I):**
        *   Path 2.1: E - F - G - H - I - J
        *   Path 2.2: E - B - H - I - J
        *   Path 2.3: E - B - C - I - J

    *   **Channel 3 (via P):**
        *   Path 3.1: E - B - C - O - P - J

2.  **Identify edge-disjoint paths to determine a lower bound for the minimum cut.**
    To find the minimum number of edges to remove (min-cut), we can look for the maximum number of edge-disjoint paths (max-flow min-cut theorem).

    Let's find three edge-disjoint paths:
    *   **Path A:** E - M - J
        Edges: (E, M), (M, J)

    *   **Path B:** E - F - G - H - I - J
        Edges: (E, F), (F, G), (G, H), (H, I), (I, J)

    *   **Path C:** E - B - C - O - P - J
        Edges: (E, B), (B, C), (C, O), (O, P), (P, J)

    Upon inspection, these three paths (Path A, Path B, and Path C) do not share any common edges. Since we have found 3 edge-disjoint paths, at least 3 edges must be removed to disconnect E from J.

3.  **Find a cut set of size 3.**
    Now that we know the minimum cut size is at least 3, we need to find a set of 3 edges whose removal disconnects E from J. A common strategy is to consider the edges directly connected to one of the target nodes that serve as "entry points" for paths from the other target node.

    Let's look at the edges connected to J:
    *   (J, M)
    *   (J, I)
    *   (J, P)
    *   (J, D)

    The edge (J, D) connects J to D. However, D is only connected to P and J. If P is disconnected from J (or if all paths to P are cut), then D cannot provide an alternative path from E to J. Therefore, (J, D) is not a critical edge for E-J connectivity if we cut the other paths.

        Let's trace from the D-side towards K or G. The nodes that act as bridges from the "D-side" (nodes like P, J, O, M, N, B, C, I, Y) to the "F-side" (nodes like K, G, X, H) are H and X.
        *   Node X is connected to H, K, G. To reach X from the D-side, one must pass through H (since K and G are on the F-side).
        *   Node H is connected to B, C, I (from the D-side) and X, G (towards the F-side).

        Therefore, any path from D to K or G (and subsequently to F) that does not pass through E, must necessarily pass through node H.
        Examples of such paths:
        *   D-J-I-H-X-K-F
        *   D-J-I-H-G-F
        *   D-P-O-N-B-H-X-K-F
        *   D-P-O-C-H-G-F

3.  **Identify the minimum cut set:**
    Based on the path analysis, all paths from D to F must pass through either node E (Group 1) or node H (Group 2).
    *   If I remove node E, all paths in Group 1 are broken. However, paths in Group 2 (e.g., D-J-I-H-G-F) would still exist.
    *   If I remove node H, all paths in Group 2 are broken. This is because all paths to K or G from the D-side must pass through H (as explained above, X also relies on H to connect to the D-side). However, paths in Group 1 (e.g., D-P-O-N-B-E-F) would still exist.

    Since removing either E or H alone is not sufficient to disconnect D and F, I need to remove both.
    Removing both E and H will break all paths in Group 1 (by removing E) and all paths in Group 2 (by removing H). This ensures no path remains between D and F.

4.  **Verify minimality:**
    As established, removing a single node (E or H or any other node) is not enough to disconnect D and F. Therefore, a cut set of size 2 is the minimum.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, H]

        *   Path 2: F - G - H - I - J
    *   These two paths are edge-disjoint. For example, Path 1 uses edges (F,E), (E,M), (M,J), while Path 2 uses edges (F,G), (G,H), (H,I), (I,J). They do not share any common edges.
    *   Since there are at least two edge-disjoint paths between F and J, it is impossible to disconnect them by removing only one edge.
    *   Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove must be at least 2.
6.  **Conclusion:** Since we found a valid cut set of size 2, and we've proven that a cut of size 1 is not possible, the minimum number of edges to remove is 2.

Final Answer: Edges to remove: 2, Cut Set: [(F, E), (F, G)]

*   Nodes X and K are only connected to F and G (and each other).
*   Node G is connected to F, X, K, and H.
*   Node E is connected to F, L, M, I, and B.

This means that any path from F to J must either go through E, or it must go through G (and then potentially H).
Let's verify this:
1.  Paths starting F -> E:
    *   F -> E -> M -> J
    *   F -> E -> L -> M -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> C -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> I -> C -> N -> O -> P -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J
    *   F -> E -> B -> N -> O -> P -> J

2.  Paths starting F -> G (or F -> K -> G, or F -> X -> K -> G):
    *   F -> G -> H -> I -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> I -> P -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> E -> M -> J (This path also uses E)
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> E -> I -> J (This path also uses E)
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> N -> C -> I -> J
    *   F -> G -> H -> B -> N -> O -> P -> J

From this analysis, it's clear that all paths from F to J must pass through either node E or node G (since X and K only lead to G or F itself).

Let's test if removing a single node can disconnect F and J:
*   If I remove E: Paths through F -> E are broken. However, paths through F -> G -> H -> I -> J (and its variations) still exist. So, E alone is not enough.
*   If I remove G: Paths through F -> G (and F -> K -> G, F -> X -> K -> G) are broken. However, paths through F -> E -> M -> J (and its variations) still exist. So, G alone is not enough.
*   If I remove H: Paths through F -> G -> H are broken. However, paths through F -> E -> M -> J (and its variations) still exist. Also, paths like F -> E -> B -> H -> I -> J would be broken, but F -> E -> I -> J would still exist. So, H alone is not enough.
*   If I remove any other single node (e.g., I, M, P, B, C, N, O, D, L, X, K, Y), there will always be multiple alternative paths remaining. For example, removing M only breaks paths through M, leaving many paths through I and P.

Since no single node can disconnect F and J, the minimum number of nodes to remove must be at least 2.

Now, let's consider removing two nodes.
Based on the observation that all paths must pass through E or G:
If I remove both E and G:
1.  All paths starting F -> E are broken because E is removed.
2.  All paths starting F -> G are broken because G is removed.
3.  Nodes X and K are only connected to F and G (and each other). Since G is removed, X and K are only connected to F.
Therefore, the cluster of nodes {F, X, K} becomes completely isolated from the rest of the graph. Since J is not in this cluster, F is disconnected from J.

This confirms that removing E and G successfully disconnects F from J. This is a cut set of size 2.

Another possible cut set of size 2 would be [E, H].
1.  If E is removed, all paths F -> E -> ... are broken.
2.  If H is removed, all paths F -> G -> H -> ... are broken. Since G is only connected to F, X, K, and H, removing H isolates G (and thus F, X, K) from the rest of the graph.
Therefore, removing E and H also disconnects F from J.

Both [E, G] and [E, H] are valid minimal cut sets of size 2.

Final Answer: Nodes to remove: 2, Cut Set: [E, G]

I will look for paths that are vertex-disjoint (except for H and N themselves) to apply Menger's Theorem, which states that the minimum number of vertices to remove to disconnect two non-adjacent vertices is equal to the maximum number of vertex-disjoint paths between them.

Let's trace paths from H to N:

1.  **Path 1: H - C - N**
    *   Intermediate node: {C}

2.  **Path 2: H - I - J - M - O - N**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {I, J, M, O}

3.  **Path 3: H - X - K - F - E - B - N**
    *   Intermediate nodes: {X, K, F, E, B}

Now, let's check if these three paths are vertex-disjoint (meaning their sets of intermediate nodes do not overlap):
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 1: {C}
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 2: {I, J, M, O}
*   Intermediate nodes for Path 3: {X, K, F, E, B}

These three sets of intermediate nodes are indeed disjoint. No node appears in more than one set.
Therefore, there are at least 3 vertex-disjoint paths between H and N. According to Menger's Theorem, the minimum number of nodes required to disconnect H and N must be at least 3.

Next, I need to find a cut set of size 3 that successfully disconnects H and N.
Based on the identified disjoint paths, a natural candidate for a cut set would be to pick one node from each path.
Let's choose:
*   C from Path 1
*   J from Path 2 (J is a bottleneck for all paths starting H-I-J)
*   K from Path 3 (K is a bottleneck for all paths starting H-X-K or H-G-K or H-G-X-K)

    *   H - I - J - D - P - Y - N
    *   H - I - J - P - Y - N
    All these paths are severed by removing J.

    All these paths are severed by removing K.

4.  **List potential node-disjoint paths from G to I:**
    *   **Path 1:** G - H - I

Step-by-step reasoning:
The target nodes are J (black) and F (black). The goal is to find the minimum number of edges to remove to completely disconnect J from F.

First, I identify all direct neighbors of F: B, E, L, X, K, G. Any path from J to F must pass through one of these nodes and then use the corresponding edge to F, or pass through one of these nodes and then through another neighbor of F to reach F.

Let's categorize the paths from J to F based on the immediate neighbors of F:

**Category 1: Paths to F via B, E, or L.**
- Paths to F via L: All paths to L from J must use the edge (L,F) to reach F. The only path to L is J-M-L. So, (L,F) is a critical edge for this branch.
- Paths to F via E: All paths to E from J must use the edge (E,F) to reach F. E is connected to B. So, paths like J-...-B-E-F exist.
- Paths to F via B: All paths to B from J must use the edge (B,F) to reach F. B is connected to O, N, C, E.

To disconnect F from all paths coming through B, E, and L, we can consider removing the edges directly connecting these nodes to F:
- (B,F)
- (E,F)
- (L,F)
Removing these 3 edges ensures that no path from J can reach F via B, E, or L.

**Category 2: Paths to F via X, K, or G.**
- Paths to F via X: All paths to X from J must use the edge (X,F) to reach F. X is connected to H and K.
- Paths to F via G: All paths to G from J must use the edge (G,F) to reach F. G is connected to H and K.
- Paths to F via K: All paths to K from J must use the edge (K,F) to reach F. K is connected to X and G.

- Paths to X: J-I-H-X, J-P-I-H-X.
- Paths to G: J-I-H-G, J-P-I-H-G.
- Paths to K: J-I-H-X-K, J-I-H-G-K, J-P-I-H-X-K, J-P-I-H-G-K.

To disconnect H from X, K, and G, we can remove the edges (H,X) and (H,G).
- Removing (H,X) cuts all paths from H to X (and subsequently to K and F via X).
- Removing (H,G) cuts all paths from H to G (and subsequently to K and F via G).
By removing these 2 edges, H is disconnected from X, K, and G. Since all paths from J to X, K, G must pass through H, this effectively disconnects J from X, K, and G.

**Combining the cuts:**
To disconnect J from F, we need to cut all paths from both categories.
From Category 1, we need to remove [(B,F), (E,F), (L,F)] (3 edges).
From Category 2, we need to remove [(H,X), (H,G)] (2 edges).

The total number of edges to remove is 3 + 2 = 5.

Let's verify that this set of 5 edges disconnects J and F:
1.  **Remove (B,F):** This cuts all direct paths from B to F.
2.  **Remove (E,F):** This cuts all direct paths from E to F, including those that came via B (e.g., J-...-B-E-F).
3.  **Remove (L,F):** This cuts all direct paths from L to F (e.g., J-M-L-F).
    At this point, all paths from J to F that pass through B, E, or L are broken.
4.  **Remove (H,X):** This cuts all paths from H to X, and thus all paths from J (via I or P-I-H) to X and then to F (e.g., J-I-H-X-F, J-I-H-X-K-F).
5.  **Remove (H,G):** This cuts all paths from H to G, and thus all paths from J (via I or P-I-H) to G and then to F (e.g., J-I-H-G-F, J-I-H-G-K-F).
    At this point, all paths from J to F that pass through H (and then X, K, or G) are broken.

Since all possible paths from J to F fall into one of these two categories, removing these 5 edges ensures complete disconnection.

**Checking for a smaller cut:**
I systematically explored combinations of 3 and 4 edges in my thought process and found that they always left at least one path intact. For example, if we try to cut only 4 edges, we would need to leave one of the "entry points" to F (B, E, L, X, K, G) connected to F, or leave a path to one of these entry points from J. The structure of the graph, with multiple distinct "channels" leading to F, requires at least 5 edges to be cut.

    *   **Scenario A: Removing 3 edges directly connected to J.**

        After removing these three edges, J is still connected to D via the edge (D, J).
        We need to check if there's any path from F to D.
        Consider the path: F - G - H - B - C - O - P - D - J.
        This path is still active because (D, J) is not removed, and the path from F to D is intact.
        Therefore, removing 3 edges directly connected to J is not sufficient. This applies to any combination of 3 edges incident to J.

    *   **Scenario B: Removing 3 edges not all directly connected to J.**
        Let's try to identify "bottleneck" edges further away from J.
        Consider the following set of 3 edges: [(E, M), (H, I), (O, P)].
        *   Removing (E, M) breaks paths like F - E - M - J. However, the path F - E - L - M - J is still active because (E, L), (L, M), and (M, J) are not removed.

    Let's identify some edge-disjoint paths:

    *   **Path 1:** H - B - N
        Edges: `(H,B)`, `(B,N)`

    *   **Path 2:** H - I - C - N
        Edges: `(H,I)`, `(I,C)`, `(C,N)`

    *   **Path 3:** H - E - M - J - P - O - N
        Edges: `(H,E)`, `(E,M)`, `(M,J)`, `(J,P)`, `(P,O)`, `(O,N)`

    *   Edges in Path 2: `{(H,I), (I,C), (C,N)}`
    *   Edges in Path 3: `{(H,E), (E,M), (M,J), (J,P), (P,O), (O,N)}`

    Upon inspection, these three sets of edges are completely disjoint. No edge is shared between any two of these paths.

4.  **Determine the minimum number of edges to remove:**

5.  **Find a cut set of size 3 and verify it:**

According to Menger's Theorem, since there are 4 edge-disjoint paths, the minimum number of edges that must be removed to disconnect F from D is at least 4.

Next, I need to find a cut set of 4 edges that actually disconnects F from D. A common strategy is to identify the "bottleneck" edges that connect the source's immediate neighborhood to the rest of the graph leading to the destination.
The nodes directly connected to F are M, L, E, G, X, K.
Nodes L, X, K are primarily connected to E, G, or M, which are also direct neighbors of F.
So, all paths from F must pass through M, E, or G to reach D.

Let's consider the edges that lead out from M, E, and G towards D:

- From E: (E, B) (E also connects to L, but L connects back to M or E, so (E,B) is the main exit from E towards D's side)
- From G: (G, H) and (G, B) (G also connects to E, X, K, but these are F-side nodes)

Let's propose the cut set: [(M, J), (E, B), (G, H), (G, B)]. This set has 4 edges.
Now, I will verify that removing these 4 edges disconnects F from D by checking all possible path types:
1.  Any path from F through M to D must use (M, J). Removing (M, J) cuts these paths (e.g., F-M-J-D, F-L-M-J-D, F-E-L-M-J-D).
2.  Any path from F through E to D that doesn't go via M (which is already cut) must use (E, B). Removing (E, B) cuts these paths (e.g., F-E-B-N-D, F-E-B-C-O-P-D, F-E-B-C-O-Y-D).
3.  Any path from F through G to D that doesn't go via B (which is covered by (G,B)) must use (G, H). Removing (G, H) cuts these paths (e.g., F-G-H-I-J-D, F-G-H-I-P-D, F-X-G-H-I-J-D, F-K-G-H-I-P-D).
4.  Any path from F through G to D that doesn't go via H (which is already cut) must use (G, B). Removing (G, B) cuts these paths (e.g., F-G-B-N-D, F-G-B-C-O-P-D, F-G-B-C-O-Y-D, F-X-G-B-N-D, F-K-G-B-C-O-Y-D).

Since all paths from F to D must pass through M, E, or G, and all outgoing connections from M, E, and G towards D are covered by these 4 edges, removing them will completely disconnect F from D.

Therefore, the minimum number of edges to remove is 4.
